   1| 3
   2| In Memoriam
   3| Marion M. Benson, 83
   4| Marion passed away on December 27, 2015 in Leominster. She was very active in the Town of
   5| Lunenburg where she lived for 40 plus years. She was an active volunteer in both church & civic
   6| groups, including the School Committee. After her return to Lunenburg in 1981, she began
   7| volunteering on the Planning Board, where she quickly became Chairperson. She volunteered for
   8| two decades on the Board at which time the position evolved into full-time Planning Director; a
   9| position she held until 2014. In May 2015, her many years of service were recognized at Annual
  10| Town Meeting by a Ritter Building conference room being named in her honor.
  11| August “Gus” S. Misner, 78
  12| Gus passed away on July 15, 2015 in Togus, Maine following a long battle with cancer.
  13| He was born in Fitchburg on December 17, 1936 where he attended school and played
  14| football. He served in the U.S. Army from 1955-1964, was stationed in the Arctic and was
  15| an aide to President Eisenhower. He later became a police officer in Lunenburg for several
  16| years until ill health forced his retirement.
  17|                Senator Mary Padula, 89
  18|        Mary Louise Padula, nee Harley, passed away on October 29, 2015. Born in Fitchburg and
  19|        nearly a lifetime resident of Lunenburg, Mary served as Town Clerk, Tax Collector and
  20|        Treasurer for 30 years. Upon leaving the Town Hall, she represented the Second Worcester
  21|        and Middlesex District in the Massachusetts Senate for 8 years, and as part of Governor
  22|        Weld’s cabinet, served as Secretary of Housing & Community Development for 5 years.
  23|        She served as President of the Massachusetts Treasurer and Tax Collector Association and
  24|        as Assistant Minority Leader in the Senate and on many boards and committees. She was
  25|        recognized by Fitchburg State College as one of the 100 citizens who made the greatest
  26|        difference in North Central Massachusetts in the last 100 years.
  27| David M. Shea, 67
  28| David Shea passed away on August 8, 2015 after a courageous battle with cancer. David
  29| was an atmospheric scientist with an environmental consulting company for 37 years.
  30| He was a longtime resident of Lunenburg. David was an original founder of the
  31| Lunenburg Recycling Commission and a member of the Board of Health for 30 years.
  32| He was a passionate golfer who enjoyed playing as well as organizing an annual golf
  33| tournament. David was a soccer coach and a high school soccer referee for many years.
  34| David was known for his great sense of humor and his unending energy. He seemed to
  35|                             know everyone and was referred to many times as the “Mayor of Lunenburg.”

===PAGE 7=== (plain, 29 lines)
   1| 7
   2| LUNENBURG
   3| Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It  occupies a  land area of
   4| approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
   5| approximately 43 miles from Boston.  It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the
   6| City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancas ter and the City of Leominster, on the east
   7| by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
   8| Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting o f all registered voters
   9| in the t own. Subject to the legislative decision s made by town meeti ng, the affairs of the t own
  10| are generally administered by a five member Board of S electmen and a  Town Manager. Local
  11| school affairs are administered by an elected seven member School Committee while local taxes
  12| are assessed by a three m ember Board of A ssessors, all are appointed on an at -large basis for
  13| staggered three-year terms.
  14| Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County.  Three water bodies
  15| account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the t own's histo rical development.  Lake
  16| Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the
  17| remainder of Lunenburg.  This district was developed as a housing base for  many factory
  18| workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster an d Fitchburg.   In many ways the
  19| characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these cities than of
  20| the rest of Lunenb urg. The Hickory Hills area of t own, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  21| developed as a private community compri sed largely of vacation cottages.  The third body of
  22| water is Shirley Re servoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages
  23| that have now been modified to year -round residences. Commercial and industrial development
  24| is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major arteries (Rout es 2A and 13),
  25| and the t own's peripheries , due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's and Fitchburg's
  26| sewer and water systems.  Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed in to single
  27| family housing, farming continues o n in Lunenburg.  At the Annual Town Meeting on May 4 th,
  28| 2013, the Town voted to establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve, and
  29| enhance farming in the community and to administer the “Right to Farm” bylaw.

===PAGE 19=== (plain, 50 lines)
   1| 19
   2| REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   3| The Town continued to make important progress in 2015 in many areas, including:  School Building;
   4| Building Reuse; Increased Public Transportation; and updating the Pavement Management Plan.    The MS/
   5| HS building is well under construction and set to open in September 2016.  Fifty-eight (58%) of the
   6| project’s eligible costs will be paid through a grant from the MA School Building Authority.   As of
   7| December 31, 2015, approximately $30M of the Town’s total share had been permanently financed in order
   8| to take best advantage of the low interest rates.
   9| The Board of Selectmen (BOS) appointed Building Reuse Committee continued its work throughout 2015.
  10| In the summer, the Committee issued a Request for Proposals for a Building Reuse a nd Space Needs Study.
  11| Town's goal for the Project was to assess the existing conditions of four (4) buildings owned by the Town
  12| and determine the most cost effective building(s) to accommodate the Town's current and anticipated space
  13| needs for Town administrative operations currently housed at Town Hall and remote sites.  The building(s)
  14| that are determined not to be advantageous to the Town may be deemed as “surplus property“ and disposed
  15| of in accordance with M.G.L. c. 30B§16.  The Assessment and Space Needs study include the following
  16| four (4) buildings:  Town Hall; Ritter Memorial Building: Thomas C. Passios Elementary; and the Brooks
  17| House.  In late summer, the Town hired Tappé & Associates, the same firm serving as Architect on the MS/
  18| HS School Project, to complete this study.  The results of the study were presented to the BOS in 2016.
  19| The BOS worked diligently with the Department of Public Works to update its existing Pavement
  20| Management Plan.  The updated plan built upon the foundation of the existing plan but included additional
  21| evaluation criteria.  The original plan was developed in 2009 and funding decisions from that point up until
  22| FY16 were based upon that plan.  Beginning in FY2017, all funding decisions will be based upon the
  23| revised plan which established a PCI, or Pavement Condition Index, for each roadway and a Prioritize List
  24| which takes into account both the functional classification of the roadway and the needed repair.  The
  25| Board further determined that a PCI of 70 would be considered a generally acceptable pavement condition
  26| and worked on determining the best funding mechanism for the PMP Program to insure that maintenance
  27| needs would be addressed uniformly across all roadway classifications.  To that end, the Board agreed to
  28| put forth a debt exemption question to the voters, seeking an additional $4M in tax dollars to complete the
  29| large backlog of needed maintenance & repair work.  The work would be completed over a period of 3 – 4
  30| years and would be financed over a period of at least twice as long.  Thereafter, the Town would endeavor
  31| to include additional funding within the Operating Budget to provide for a sufficient level of funding to
  32| maintain roads at a PCI of 70.
  33| The Town also continued to work on its largest and most comprehensive roadway project, the
  34| redevelopment of Summer Street, a project slated to begin in fall of 2016.  In 2014, the Town committed
  35| $230,312 from Chapter 90 Funding to fund the Town’s portion of the 75% design.  The project is expected
  36| to cost about $7M, all of which will be funded though the Regional Transportation Improvement Plan
  37| (TIP), which is a grant of State & Federal funds.  The project was approved for TIP funding for FFY2017.
  38| One of the most exciting projects which continued into 2015 was the implementation of enhanced public
  39| transportation within Lunenburg.  The Town pays both an annual assessment to the MART and to the
  40| MBTA.  The assessment to MART pays for local/ regional transportation.  The assessment to the MBTA
  41| pays for access to the commuter rail system in Fitchburg and Leominster.  The Town learned through
  42| MART that there is a provision in State Law that would allow the Town to use its MBTA Assessment to
  43| purchase additional local transportation, at no additional cost to the taxpayer.  Accor dingly, the Town
  44| undertook a review of residents’ transportation needs and began working with MART to provide additional
  45| local transportation.  The additional services began in spring 2015, though ridership was low.  The Town
  46| was able to revise its transportation offerings to include limited service along the full length of Route 2A,
  47| from the town center to the Shirley line.  The Town will continue to monitor ridership and make
  48| adjustments as needed to best service the public.
  49| I would be remiss if I did not take this opportunity to thank all of the people in this town who give of their
  50| time and many talents to make Lunenburg a better place.  Thank you all for your dedicated service.

===PAGE 36=== (plain, 14 lines)
   1| 36
   2| BOARD OF ASSESSORS
   3| The Board of Assessors saw its first transition in a number of years.  Elected to the Board twenty-one years ago,
   4| Christopher Comeau chose not to seek re-election.  Chris was a reliable and respected member who could be
   5| counted on to support the office in its long-standing mission to improve the office, render fair tax assessments, and
   6| treat the taxpaying public well.  Never shying away from offering opinions over the Town’s financial state, he
   7| sought to find balance between financial prudence and supporting worthwhile projects.
   8| Replacing Mr. Comeau on the Board is Richard Letarte, a prominent accountant and businessman.   Mr. Letarte
   9| brings considerable experience and time to work on special assignments.
  10| The Assessors’ administrative staff, Louise Paquette and Sheila Craigen, along with the professional assessment
  11| staff of Regional Resource Group, Inc. (RRG) led by Harald Scheid round out the office.  RRG veteran assessor
  12| Rebecca Boucher replaced George Bourgault as Associate Regional Assessor.
  13| The office anticipates a busy 2016 calendar year.  An improving economy will see the construction of more homes
  14| and apartment dwellings.

===PAGE 49=== (plain, 36 lines)
   1| 49
   2| Agricultural Commission
   3|  The Agricultural Commission was created in 2013 with the aim of encouraging the continued
   4| pursuit of agriculture in Lunenburg, promoting agriculturally-based economic opportunities, providing an
   5| opportunity for mediation and negotiation of farming issues, educating the community and advocating for
   6| the continued understanding of the importance of locally produced food and farm products.
   7|             With this continued understanding and the continued support of the community, we can work
   8| toward the preservation of prime agricultural lands, which serve to beautify Lunenburg visually and
   9| maintain the rural charm wherein Lunenburg’s appeal lies.
  10|             The Agricultural Commission has had the opportunity to mediate several issues concerning
  11| farming and has tried through education and investigation to help the parties involved reach equitable
  12| arrangements within the existing laws and protections.
  13|             We have provided information to prospective buyers of agricultural property in town as well as
  14| providing guidance to residents preparing to go before town commissions.  We are currently adding links
  15| to information and services from the Agricultural Commission website.  With the strong support of the
  16| Board of Selectmen, the Agricultural Commission was able to substantially expand the Lunenburg
  17| Community Farmers’ Market, offering a wider variety of vendors, products and musical entertainment
  18| from the local area.
  19|             The Agricultural Commission would like to express heartfelt thanks to the Farmers’ Market site
  20| management team, to the participating vendors, the entertainers (usually paid in produce!) and especially
  21| to the members of the community who have shown a strong and consistent support of the market and the
  22| local farmers who depend on community patronage.
  23| The Agricultural Commission is:
  24| Jeffrey L. Mendoza, Chairman,
  25| Garrett Stillman, Vice-Chairman
  26| Maureen Dupuis, Clerk
  27| Douglas MacMillan, Member
  28| Renee Trakimas, Member
  29| The Commission would also like to express tremendous gratitude to our Planning Board liaison, Mr.
  30| Matthew Allison for his tireless work in providing maps, regulations, bylaw references and research to the
  31| Agricultural Commission.
  32| Thank you to the Town of Lunenburg for your continued support of the Agricultural Commission and its
  33| mission toward preserving the agricultural fabric of our town.
  34| Respectfully Submitted,
  35| Jeffrey L. Mendoza, Chairman
  36| Lunenburg Agricultural Commission

===PAGE 50=== (plain, 32 lines)
   1| 50
   2| BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   3| The Building Departmen t is located  on the second floor of the  Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
   4| Avenue, Lunenburg, MA .  Office hours for the Building Department are M onday and Wednesday 8:00 A.M. to
   5| 4:00 P.M., Tues day and Thursday 8:00 A.M. to 6:00 P.M. The Building Department  is closed on Fridays.
   6| Michael J. Sauvageau serves as the Building Commissioner , John Morreale serves as the  Assistant Building
   7| Inspector and Lisa Normandin is the department Administrative Assistant.
   8| The Building Department now processes all Wiring, Plumbing and Gas permits, a duty that was formerly done in
   9| the Office of the Town Clerk.
  10| Building permits for 2015 decreased slightly from 481 in 2014 to 452 for 2015 but still reflects increased
  11| construction activity since the tail end of the economic and housing downturn a couple of years back. Solar
  12| permits remain steady as does new single family homes and townhouses. The Emerald Place project should be
  13| completed this year. Several large projects are in the reviewing stage or have been approved and are anticipated to
  14| begin construction soon. The new high school is progressing well and it appears it will be completed on or before
  15| schedule.
  16| The 2009 International Building Code for residential and commercial construction is the current building code
  17| and it is anticipated that a new Ninth Edition Code (2015 International Building Code) will be in effect by June of
  18| this year.
  19| Residents and developers are reminded that Lunenburg is a “Stretch Code Community” which has specific
  20| code requirements in addition to the current energy code requirements.
  21| Carbon monoxide detectors are required by law to be installed in every home regardless of the age of the
  22| dwelling.  Also, all swimming pools are required to obtain a building permit and an electrical permit.  Inflatable
  23| pools that do not have code required fencing around them do not meet the building code and should be removed.
  24| Residents should be aware that inflatable swimming pools do not meet code compliance  requirements.  P lease
  25| contact our office to determine what steps need to take place for compliance and safety.
  26| Residents should be aware that Massachusetts Law requires that all persons, partnerships and corporations who
  27| bid or perform residential contracting (reconstruction, alterations, additions etc.) for work exc eeding $1000.00 be
  28| registered with the Commonwealth.  This office will enforce this provision, however you should ask your
  29| contractor about his/her registration.  Please contact this office with any questions about this requirement or call
  30| for any information regarding zoning requirements or building code issues.
  31| Michael J. Sauvageau
  32| Building Commissioner & Zoning Official

   2| Conservation Commission
   3| The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven member board appointed to 3-year terms
   4| by the Board of Selectmen, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act M.G.L.
   5| Chapter 40, § 8C. The Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act M.G.L. Chapter
   6| 131, § 40 and Article XXII of the local bylaws for the protection of wetlands.
   7| The year 2015 was one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and meetings for
   8| a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep
   9| projects. The Conservation Commission worked to spell out goals of preparing to use the year 2016 to
  10| revise its regulations to provide consistency with the original by-law and to find those sections that may
  11| be out of date and need to be modified.
  12| The Commission held numerous inspections with its agent on weekends prior to meetings to familiarize
  13| themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The Commission is working
  14| with the Town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates from the EPA and
  15| working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification.
  16| The Conservation Commission in cooperation with the Open Space Committee and the Lunenburg Snow
  17| Riders Club has closed out a grant that obtained the former Lane property as Open Space and has worked
  18| to place an adequate and American Disabilities Act- compliant parking area that would blend in with the
  19| trail heads on Holman Street. The Snow Riders continue to obtain grant monies to continue to improve
  20| trail access to be enjoyed by all. The Conservation Commission held public recreational walks through its
  21| open space parcels and plans to continue to do so.
  22| This year also marked the retirement of Janice Carrier, who worked diligently with the Conservation
  23| Commission as the Administrator for the past 10 years. The Commission would like to take this
  24| opportunity to thank her for her tremendous efforts and wishes her a healthy and happy retirement!
  25| Currently Consulting Agent Matthew S. Marro is manning the office in both capacities taking Janice’s
  26| place. But no one will ever fill her shoes!
  27| The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and looks forward to many more!
  28| Respectfully Submitted,
  29| Richard Bursch, Chair
  30| Jack Rabbitt, Vice-Chair
  31| Brad Kennedy
  32| Tom Bertram
  33| Carl Luck
  34| Todd Dwyer
  35| Kenneth Jones
  36| Matthew S. Marro, Agent

   1| 53
   2| COUNCIL ON AGING
   3| The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   4| supportive services, programs and education.  Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts
   5| General Law Chapter 40, § 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or
   6| services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   7| The FY15 Council on Aging staff consisted of Doreen C. Noble, Director; Susan Doherty, Administrative
   8| Assistant and Transportation; Faith A. Anderson, Outreach Coordinator; Joanne Brazell, Ruth Swiecicki,
   9| and Jeanne Tatro,  Meal Site Coordinators; Judy Alario and Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants; Gerry
  10| Beloin and Jim McGuigan, MART Van Drivers.
  11| The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed by the Lunenburg Board of
  12| Selectmen.
  13| The FY15 Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln,  Vice-Chairperson Deb
  14| Seeley, Betty DiGiacomo, Jacquelyn Dwyer, Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin McNally,
  15| Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, and Judith Tarbell.
  16| The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House
  17| Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501(c)3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of
  18| Massachusetts, and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
  19| We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House.  In FY15 we had 41 volunteers who
  20| contributed 2,587.25 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on
  21| Wheels Program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, office help, library, and friendly visitors.  We greatly
  22| appreciate their service.
  23| The Eagle House offered a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts,
  24| intergenerational, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, and social events both at the senior
  25| center and offsite.
  26| In FY15 the Council on Aging delivered 4898 Meals on Wheels to 50 clients and provided 3,169 rides to
  27| 62 clients on our senior van.  Our combined events and service statistics totaled 10,060 units with 827
  28| participants.

===PAGE 54=== (plain, 18 lines)
   1| 54
   2| LUNENBURG CULTURAL COUNCIL
   3| (Fiscal Year 2015)
   4| The Lunenburg Cultural Council is part of a network of 329 Local Cultural Councils serving all
   5| 351 cities and towns in the Commonwealth. The LCC Program is the largest grassroots cultural
   6| funding network in the nation, supporting thousands of community-based projects in the arts,
   7| sciences and humanities every year. The state legislature provides an annual appropriation to the
   8| Massachusetts Cultural Council, a state agency, which then allocates funds to each community.
   9| The Lunenburg Cultural Council received and will distribute $4,300 in grants. These funds are
  10| granted to qualified individuals and organizations to support cultural projects that benefit the
  11| community. These grants can support a variety of artistic projects and activities in Lunenburg
  12| including exhibits, festivals, field trips, short-term artist residencies or performances in schools,
  13| workshops and lectures.
  14| The LCC met December 3, 2014 at the Lunenburg Public Library to decide grant distribution for
  15| this year's cycle (2015). All rejection and acceptance letters were mailed by December 30th.
  16| Applications for FY’2016 were due by October 15, 2015.
  17| More information about the Local Cultural Council Program is available online at
  18| http://www.mass-culture.org/lcc_public.aspx.

===PAGE 55=== (plain, 47 lines)
   1| 55
   2| DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   3| The Department  of Public W orks combines  the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery,
   4| Sewer, and Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and
   5| scheduling personnel and equipment t o best serve  the residents of the community th rough the effective
   6| delivery of an extensive scope of services with a high degree of efficiency.
   7| DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
   8| “Public Works is either working or on call twenty four hours a day three hundred and si xty five
   9| days a year to respond to any weather, infrastructure,  roadway, or facility related emergency that may
  10| arise.”
  11| We strive to enhance the awareness of the value of Public Works provides to our Town, and
  12| ensure others are aware of our contribution. T his is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make
  13| sure efforts are not forgotten as they merge into the fabric of our everyday service.
  14| Major events over the past years have highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at the
  15| table and is a major player in emergency management. Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that
  16| Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required services of others. We must continue to build on
  17| this awareness.
  18| This department must also reach out to the  public we serve and educate them to the fact that the
  19| common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of
  20| individuals, service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collectio n
  21| and recycling operations, tree removal, storm water management facilities and grounds maintenance,
  22| winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries and parks are the expected services supporting everyone in the
  23| community.
  24| We will work to engage the public in orde r to set reasonable expectations and gather support for
  25| annual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  26| PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  27| The u pdated Pavement Management Plan , inclusive of an electronic database  and a revised
  28| Town-wide pavement defect analysis. For a second time, a complete survey of every road was completed.
  29| The amount of new paving does not keep up with the seasonal deterioration factor, and as such the
  30| backlog in excess of nine million dollars. The program remains underfunded.
  31| Road Projects typically extend over two construction seasons and  overlap from one fiscal year to
  32| the next. During this year the following roads were addressed either totally or in part: Howard St.,
  33| Reservoir Rd., Goodrich St., and smaller sections of Arbor, Page, and Kilburn Sts.
  34| Documentation provided through a “pot hole” log  ( a daily record of road work specific to
  35| patching and filling of pot holes) proved that one third of all the available work days during the season are
  36| dedicated to patching and other temporary repairs to similar roadway failures. As defects are identified we
  37| respond to every location in a timely manner.  This has proven to be as less than effective  method of road
  38| repair. New pot holes appear each spring particularly after a severe winter, and often staff addresses  the
  39| same areas year after year.
  40| The seasonal crack sealing program has been effective in some locations however this application
  41| is designed as a temporary solution with an effective life span of around five years. Many streets that have
  42| been “sealed” are well beyond that time frame. This process involves the installation of rubberized asphalt
  43| under high pressure to cracks or separations in the p avement. The Pavement Management Plan identifies
  44| specific areas where this method is the best option.   This application does not take the place of curb to
  45| curb paving, only postpones it and is not applicable in areas of more serious defects.
  46| The revised Pavement plan, no matter how detailed, is of little value without an identified source of
  47| funding. Lack of revenue continues to be the major detriment to improving the overall condition of our

===PAGE 56=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 56
   2| roadways. The primary source s of funding are  the Massachusetts Department of Transportation
   3| Chapter 90 program  and a dedicated line in the DPW budg et.  The estimated value of our 86 miles of
   4| paved stree ts is, by industry standards, calculated to be  62 million dollars. The General Highway
   5| maintenance line within the operational budget  has remained virtually unchanged  for two decades while
   6| the overall omnibus budget has nearly tripled to a value in excess of 30 million dollars. In order to initiate
   7| and sustain a road improvement program a substantial inf usion of funds is necessary.   The conservative
   8| estimate remains at  approximately 1.2 million dollars  per year total or an additional $800,000.00 to
   9| existing allocations. Applying $400,000-$500,000 a year to a backlog of work that approaches 10 million
  10| dollars dooms the current plan to failure. As an example: many roads are compromised to the degree that
  11| only a major reconstruction can bring them to an acceptable quality and less travelled, local roads remain
  12| out of reach.
  13| FLEET MAINTENANCE
  14| The DPW is respon sible for the repairs  and preventative maintenance to  all Town vehicles and
  15| equipment. High performance police units,  First responder Fire and Rescue apparatus,  park, cemetery,
  16| and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by department
  17| personnel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more fr equent and therefore more costly .
  18| Due to the volatile global oil market, the overall cost of fuel and petroleum based products continues to be
  19| a moving target and therefore difficult to estimate annually.
  20| The installation of a vehicle maintenance software program has proven to be a valuable tool. Each
  21| repair is logged by department and vehicle identification number and memorialized in a data bank. Every
  22| vehicle can be tracked for part numbers, required services , as well as frequency and cost of repairs over
  23| the life of the equipment.
  24| FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  25| The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
  26| DPW Garage 520 Chase Road  Public Safety Complex
  27| Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.              Town Hall
  28| Cemetery Bldg., Holman St.              Eagle House Senior Center
  29| Town Beach    Town Common
  30| Ritter Bldg.                Teen Center
  31| Old Primary School                          Veterans Park
  32| All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding school properties
  33| The ever increasing workload made necessary the addition of a second individual to ass ist with
  34| repairs and maintenance. The increase in staff by only one has significantly lessened the need for
  35| outsourcing, specifically preventative maintenance . Staff remains on -call 24 hours a day 365 days per
  36| year.
  37| Staff keeps buildings compliant and prot ects the safe running of the work environment. Any
  38| significant interruption of service within Town buildings  carries the potential of disrupting or disabling
  39| necessary services provided to the community. The facilities support staff  are problem solvers and
  40| technicians with a wide range of experience encompassing all  of the construction trades. They deliver
  41| services or oversee those functions provided by specialized subc ontractors. Staff manages and forecasts
  42| all of the building assets and prioritizes replac ement costs.   With the use of building maintenance
  43| software, requests for service are acknowledged immediately, prioritized, and completed in a timely
  44| manner. Those individuals requesting service are contacted upon completion of the tasks a history of the
  45| repair is filed electronically.

===PAGE 57=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 57
   2| ENVIRONMENTAL
   3| Storm Water
   4| For yet another year  the Environmental Protection Agency has not  set the requirements for the
   5| next phase of storm water regulation and permits. As we aw ait the potential of what will be an  unfunded
   6| Federal mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol.
   7| The DPW , as a result of limited funding  is forced to become reactionary to roadway drainage
   8| issues. A capital program in the 1990’s earmarked $20,000.00 annually tow ard rehabilitation/installation
   9| of piping and catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding placed the financial burden upon the
  10| overall General Highway maintenance allocatio n effectively reducing drainage repairs. In anticipation of
  11| the new regulations the Town Manager has re -established a budget line within t he DPW dedicated to
  12| storm water, a necessary first step.
  13| As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are
  14| addressed. Little or no preventative maintenance, short of  public safety issues in the form of flooding or
  15| structural failure, street sweeping and  catch basin cleaning can be  initiated due t o a lack of available
  16| resources and the absences of a Town wide drainage survey. New storm water regulations will likely
  17| force the Town to become more proactive albeit with no identified source of funding. At the conclusion of
  18| winter and again in the late fall drainage structures in close proximity to open water are cleaned. Other
  19| structures throughout the Town are addressed as time and resources allow on an intermittent schedule.
  20| Waste Water
  21| The DPW continues as the caretak er of the entire sewer system. In an effort to become more
  22| efficient the Sewer Commission has outsourced much of the day to day inspections and preventative
  23| maintenance of the overall wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations.  Business management and
  24| oversight of the subcontractor remain the responsibility of the DPW.
  25| Tree Removal
  26| While it may seem difficult to believe, the infamous “Ice Storm of ‘08” continues to detrimentally
  27| affect trees in the Town right -of-way. As presented to the Board of Selectman during the initial recovery
  28| process, the “heartwood” of a large volume of trees was affected. Previously unnoticed damage together
  29| with natural att rition of every species  creates a backlog of tree felling  and pruning that far exceeds the
  30| allotted budget. The department is unable to fulfill each resident request and must prioriti ze work based
  31| upon the calculated degree of danger to residents and prope rty. A more efficient approach would involve
  32| a survey of all Town owned trees with a generated list of those requiring attention. Until a plan is in place,
  33| the DPW will act in a reactionary mode. Al l work aside from brush removal, chipping, and the felling  of
  34| small diameter trees is outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket truck and a licensed professional. Each
  35| spring DPW staff initiates the activity of removing and disposing of woody debris within the right-of-way
  36| and a Town-wide program of curbside mowing.
  37| Landfill
  38| The “capped” landfill off Young’s Rd. remains  in compliance with  the inspection protocol
  39| mandated by  the Division of Enviro nmental Protection.  The site is monitored  regularly for excessive
  40| methane gas as well as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  41| The frequency of the testing was reduced through a special permit acquired as a result of
  42| consistently favorable results.  The landfill site including the  perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular
  43| schedule by DPW personnel.

===PAGE 58=== (plain, 46 lines)
   1| 58
   2| Adjacent to  the “cap” is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both
   3| determined by the EPA  to be hazardous waste. We are permitted to store these materials through the
   4| procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires  yearly testing of these
   5| materials.
   6| On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage the
   7| annual composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents
   8| and add a valuable component to our recycling program.  There has to date been no dedicated funding
   9| toward the organization or long range remediation of this location. Once again, dollars will be taken from
  10| the General Highway line to maintain  this location while keeping it environm entally safe. Next year, a
  11| proposal to dispose of accumulated brush and trees  will be submitted to the Capital Planning Committee
  12| for consideration in order to maintain the required space for resident disposal. The accumulation of leaves
  13| is formed into long manageable rows that are turned regularly and will over time revert to compost.
  14| Solid Waste
  15| Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside
  16| collection of trash and recyclables by our current vendor, Casella Inc.
  17| Lake Shirley Dam
  18| In the early 1990’s, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley Association turned
  19| over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total  reconstruction, responsibilities for
  20| maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from
  21| the Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
  22| establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella
  23| of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates
  24| compliance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a
  25| professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a
  26| positive report.
  27| Winter Operations
  28| Snow and ice present a major challenge to our Town. The safety of the general public depends on
  29| the ability of the DPW to maintain our roadways in a safe condition despite the severity or the duration of
  30| snow and ice events. To that end we develop a winter operations procedu re based upon a detailed  plan
  31| that must be flexible due to the unpredictability of each storm. A consistently high level of service is
  32| expected and must be delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.
  33| Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan.  Financial
  34| support is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of services due to
  35| breakdowns and  repairs. Many of the front line vehicles are well beyond the optimum time for
  36| replacement. I n the fall, salt and sand are delivered to the Chase Rd. facility . Lunenburg is in a
  37| consortium with 14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price for salt.
  38| Individually the Town also solicits bids for road sand. From late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance
  39| staff inspects and prepares the trucks, sanders and plows for winter op erations. During the construction
  40| season road crews address right -of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to
  41| eliminate obstacles to plowing. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations. Typically
  42| DPW staff combines with 18-20 subcontractors to provide service.
  43| Generally it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the overall
  44| cost. For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration
  45| freezing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the department operates in a
  46| deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter.

===PAGE 59=== (plain, 21 lines)
   1| 59
   2| PARKS
   3| In conjunction with the three ele cted Park Commissioners, DPW staff oversees and maintains all
   4| parks and grounds exclusive of school pro perty. A full-time Park employee  was replaced with seasonal
   5| workers. Much of the Town-wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, resulting in a significant savings.
   6| The multi-use track complex is in dire need of repair. Funding, as well as an y organized effort between
   7| the Commissioners and all users of the facility  is sadly lacking. I am hopeful as I continue the effort
   8| toward sustainability of the field and track
   9| CEMETERY
  10| One Cemetery Division Superintendent , a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and
  11| South cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three
  12| members of the Cemetery Commission.  The DPW supplies equipment and suppo rt staff for, on average,
  13| forty burials a year.
  14| WITH RESPECT
  15| As always, I am deeply appreciative  of the continued patience and support of our residents and
  16| fellow Town employees as we persevere through these difficult times.
  17| “As caretakers of the Town’ s infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of
  18| the Town of Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations
  19| associated with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works.”
  20| Jack Rodriquenz
  21| DPW Director

   2| Information Technology Director
   3| The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through
   4| the use of technology.  The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to
   5| communicate and collaborate, manage daily tasks, organize, store, and share information, and
   6| generally conduct Town business more effectively.  We also provide the necessary infrastructure,
   7| equipment, software, and expertise to comply with current regulations and methodology when
   8| interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
   9| We have almost an entirely new staff this year, with only the director remaining constant from the
  10| previous year.
  11| Steve Malandrinos – Information Technology Director
  12|  Hired in December, 2012
  13|  Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA
  14| and Belchertown Public Schools
  15| Daniel Nadareski – Network Administrator
  16|  Retired police officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on the job
  17|  Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration, video
  18| security system administration, and IP phone system administration
  19| Jordan Pochini – Tech Support Specialist
  20|  Recently graduated from Fitchburg State University with a degree in Computer Science
  21|  Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in retail
  22| environments
  23| Along with serving as helpdesk for all users’ daily technology issues and the usual maintenance
  24| and scheduled replacement of the town’s and schools’ technology assets, the IT Department has
  25| been involved with several high impact projects throughout the past fiscal year.  The following
  26| examples are among the more noteworthy improvements that IT has implemented.
  27| An enterprise-style Wi-Fi solution has been deployed at Town Hall, the Public Library and Ritter
  28| Memorial Building.  Replacing older, consumer grade equipment has given us universal coverage
  29| within the buildings.  The system also offers an open guest network enabling visitors to access the
  30| internet, while simultaneously providing a secure connection to internal networks and services for
  31| Town employees.
  32| A large portion of the Technology department’s time this year has been dedicated to preparation
  33| for the opening of our new middle/high school.  We have had many meetings, discussions,
  34| planning sessions, and product demonstrations to make sure we deliver the absolute best
  35| experience for our students, teachers and administration while providing the highest value per tax
  36| dollar spent.
  37| We continue to strive to leverage limited resources to improve efficiency wherever possible.   I
  38| am very happy to continue in this role, and I couldn’t be more pleased with the level of skill, hard
  39| work and dedication of my staff, Daniel and Jordan.  I know that because of them we will
  40| continue to improve the technology landscape in Lunenburg while maintaining the positive
  41| reputation we’ve strived for over the past several years.

   2| LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)
   3| FY15
   4| Mission: Develop, modernize, establish and operate the Town
   5| of Lunenburg’ s Public and Government (P&G) Access Channels
   6| to reflect what matters to Lunenburg
   7| Public Access Cable Committee (PACC)
   8| Steve Walker, Chair
   9| Jeff Bajko, Secretary
  10| Faith Beall, Member
  11| The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the
  12| long-term plan to manage the Public Access Channels available to the
  13| Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town’s only cable-service
  14| provider.
  15| The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to
  16| acquire, record, broadcast and re-broadcast programming from both town
  17| functions and meetings, as well as programming from publically available
  18| sources.
  19| Programming
  20| Public Access Cable (PAC) produced 80% of the local programming on Ch9
  21| in FY15.  This included local high school sports, band concerts and Talkin’
  22| Baseball just to name a few.  The remaining 20% of programming was
  23| obtained through other media sources and online downloads.

===PAGE 63=== (plain, 16 lines)
   1| 63
   2| All content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally produced.  In addition to several live
   3| weekly municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other examples include committee/department
   4| PSAs and the Kinder Morgan Pipeline Hearings.
   5| Town departments are encouraged to take advantage of PAC to improve town government to
   6| community information flow.
   7| A third channel for education is being considered for FY17, as we move forward with the Comcast
   8| contract negotiations in 2017.  This channel would bring educational content to the community
   9| provided by local schools and other educational sources.
  10| Studio and Equipment Upgrades
  11| PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013.
  12| We have assisted members of the community to produce a number of shows in FY15 and contin-
  13| ue to encourage the public to take advantage of the facilities offered.
  14| The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming Fiscal years includes acquiring new studio space
  15| to provide a better environment for producing programming.  Part of the plan is to purchase
  16| more cameras and

===PAGE 64=== (plain, 21 lines)
   1| 64
   2| equipment for loan to increase public participation in creating content. In FY15 cameras were
   3| loaned out to parents looking to videotape sports.  We would like to increase visibility to the public
   4| to encourage more loaning of equipment, studio usage and submitting of videos from the public.
   5| The Town Hall, which houses the video server/playback system, and acts as a control room for live
   6| meeting broadcasts, received a much needed upgrade of equipment in FY15.  New HD cameras
   7| were added to the Town Hall Bilotta meeting room, as well as new camera controllers and a switch-
   8| er.  This upgrade allowed PAC to provide higher quality broadcasts and help to prepare the station
   9| for HD broadcasts when Comcast makes it available.  Elimination of old equipment in the control
  10| room has also reduced energy and space requirements.
  11| This equipment upgrade accounted for 25% of the FY15 expenses.
  12| A new video server is being considered for FY16/FY17 to continue the necessary upgrades.
  13| Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  14| In FY15, there was one full-time Program Coordinator employee and 4 part timers, classified as ven-
  15| dors.  The Program Coordinator managed the day to day operation of the organization, including all
  16| production and programming schedules, equipment and services procurement, studio and invento-
  17| ry administration, advertising, and social media. Vendors provide video recording of committee
  18| meetings and additional video assignments as needed.
  19| Of the 80% of locally produced shows on Ch9, several were produced by members of the communi-
  20| ty.  Our Producers created Talkin’ Baseball, Fitness with Wayne, Yoga with Linda, Art for Life and
  21| the LaTabella cooking show.

   3| A videographer, classified as a consultant, mainly recorded LHS sports and events, as well as doing studio
   4| production. Sports produced by the videographer accounted for over 30% of Channel 9 programming.
   5| There were no volunteer assignments in FY15, although there is an ongoing campaign to obtain volunteers
   6| to help with production.
   7| Manpower (salaries, vendors and consultant) accounted for 45% of the FY15 expenses.

   2| Lunenburg Public Library
   3| The Lunenburg Library is alive and well.  Since last May we have seen lots of changes. Karen Kemp,
   4| Children’s Librarian for 29 years, retired.  She is currently enjoying her new life which gives her more
   5| time for her art (she is a painter) and travel. Kate McCarron who served in this role for more than 24 years
   6| retired in March.  She is enjoying her time with her new grandson.  We were able to hire a new Young
   7| Adult Librarian, Nicole Piermarini.  Nicole has a Master’s Degree in Information Science and lots of
   8| experience in the Young Adult realm.  She herself is a Dr. Who fan and is an avid gamer.  Debra Laffond
   9| joined us in September to become our new Children’s Librarian. Debra filled this role in Pelham for
  10| almost 10 years.  She was named Children’s Librarian of the Year in New Hampshire in 2013 for her
  11| exceptional work.  The staff and Library Trustees are thrilled to have Nicole and Debra here at the LPL.
  12| Sandy Altucher, our first ever Digital Services Librarian, left in late fall.  Sandy’s very personable style
  13| and technical expertise is sorely missed.  We also hired two new pages, Sage Scorse and Robin Morris,
  14| they have become trusted assets in the Library.
  15| The Board of Trustees said goodbye to Jeanne Raboin and Joanne McQuaid.  We thank them for their
  16| time and energy in helping us. Kiirja Paananen, Donna Saiia and Susan Visser were welcomed to the
  17| Board.
  18| The Library building celebrated its 10th anniversary in December.  Lots of folks that were intimately
  19| involved with the fundraising and the building of this Library returned to help us celebrate – even the
  20| architect, J. Robert Stewart stopped by.  It was a wonderful heartfelt gathering.
  21| Changes to the building this year included a new burner (which the staff really enjoyed this winter) and
  22| the migration of exterior lighting to LED, a more environmentally efficient option.  Our thanks to the
  23| Lunenburg’s Green Community Task Force for the funding and the help in getting this done.  We are
  24| hoping to receive a grant to allow us to migrate the interior lights to LED as well.   We also painted the
  25| café area and the Teen Room.  We feel very lucky that we have the Friends organization, the Board of
  26| Trustees and the DPW who really take a lot of pride in how the Library looks and operates.
  27| In terms of technology we have upgraded our Wi-Fi network and added a general Facebook page for a
  28| total of three Facebook pages.  We have also added three new EReaders and tablets that can be borrowed.
  29| The Library now has a “real” telescope that can also be borrowed, as well as a volleyball kit that has
  30| everything in it if you would like to play.
  31| One thing we have focused on this year is more community outreach.  We know that in order for the
  32| Library to maintain its position in Lunenburg we have to provide relevant resources to the community –
  33| whether that be programs or library materials.  We are trying hard to connect with the different
  34| community organizations and assist them in whatever way we can. A couple of examples are the
  35| Lunenburg Historical Society, who is now having their meetings here at the Library with the Library
  36| helping them to advertise; we also have reached out to area preschools and they are now coming to the
  37| Library on a weekly basis; and our YA Librarian and Brianna Lively at the High School have joined
  38| forces and resources to share an Anime Club.
  39| We have really increased our programs this year – in all areas.  We certainly spanned a broad range of
  40| interests from a Quidditch match in the main room of the Library to Ukulele lessons to an exhibition of
  41| hundreds of fossils and so much more.
  42| The staff and Trustees of the Lunenburg Library would like to thank all of our hard working volunteers,
  43| they are part of the fabric of the Library and they make so many things possible: Bea Adams, Sandy
  44| Flanagan, Barbara Fleming, Doug Fleming, Ruth Hamilton, Carol Gaudet, Karen Godin, and Ann Smith.

   2| Thoughts from Nicole Piermarini, Young Adult (YA) Librarian:
   3| A new chapter started in the teen room this year, beginning with the retirement of Kate McCarron after 24
   4| years of service.  And while she will be dearly missed, we are very excited to start this new chapter and
   5| add to what Kate began.
   6| Upon entering the teen room, you will notice some changes and a new layout. The computers all have
   7| new adjustable chairs, suited for the high counters.  There is a graffiti bulletin board in the corner hangout
   8| area, complete with chalk markers. Also, there is a new gaming center right as you walk into the room.
   9| We purchased a Wii U console and games to create a kit that teens can borrow at the main desk and play
  10| in house on the new television.  The new layout has attracted new teens to the Library and it is now one of
  11| THE places to be after school.
  12| The space isn’t the only part of the room to get a face lift.  We are slowly examining the collection,
  13| removing damaged books, updating and replacing them as necessary in an effort to ensure we have the
  14| most up-to-date and responsive collection possible.  The manga collection has undergone a massive
  15| overhaul in an effort to create space and to make it more current.  Series are being completed and new
  16| popular ones are being added.  In the fiction collection, we are relabeling our series to include order
  17| number in an effort to make them easier to find.  As each section is completed, it has been given new
  18| signage to draw attention and interest.
  19| 2015 was a year in transition and we will continue to examine, update, and strive to make the teen room
  20| the best, most responsive space it can be.
  21| Thoughts from Debra Laffond, Children’s Librarian
  22| The year 2015 saw the end of an era in the Lunenburg Public Library- beloved Children’s Librarian
  23| Karen Kemp retired after 29 years.  While she will be missed, we were very excited to make some
  24| changes to enhance what Karen had built up over the years.
  25| As you enter the Children’s Area, you will notice that we have lots of new toys and manipulatives to
  26| support the children’s development of reading and social skills.   We are striving to make each visit to the
  27| library a fun filled, stimulating experience.  We are examining the collection to weed outdated, damaged
  28| items and to assess areas of need to ensure that we are providing our patrons with the best, most current
  29| information that we can.  We are rearranging certain areas of the collection to make it easier for patrons to
  30| find what they are looking for, to focus attention on some of the exciting new series and other items that
  31| we have added, and to generate excitement about our beautiful Children’s Area!   We have created some
  32| tools with the goal of making it easier for families to choose materials for their children. We have
  33| increased labeling and signage to point to certain new items, we have totally overhauled our children
  34| board book collection and have added many new titles, we have created “book bundles” that are designed
  35| for grab and go for busy patrons.
  36| We will continue to examine and revamp as we strive to keep the LPL viable and exciting in the lives of
  37| Lunenburg residents for many years to come!  As Alan Watts said, “The only way to make sense out of
  38| change is to plunge into it, move with it, and join the dance.”

===PAGE 69=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 69
   2| SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   3| Est. 1998
   4| CALANDAR YEAR JANUARY 1, 2015 – NOVEMBER 1, 2015
   5| “A unique approach to balance the social & financial needs of senior homeowners and local governments
   6| of Massachusetts” Archhana Prakash, Post Doctoral Fellow of Population Studies at the Center of
   7| University of Michigan
   8| The Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a 5 member committee appointed by the Lunenburg Board of
   9| Selectmen.
  10| The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens who are age 60
  11| plus an opportunity to receive up to $1,000 off their property tax bill in the calendar year rather than the
  12| fiscal year.  Participants worked in various town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general
  13| office duties, custodial, library shelving, and  kitchen assistance.
  14| For each hour worked a person received $10 and worked up to 100 hours between January 1, 2015 and
  15| November 1, 2015 to receive the abatement.  Any hours worked beyond that were considered volunteer
  16| hours.
  17| There were nine Lunenburg residents who participated between January 1, 2015 and November 1, 2015.
  18| These individuals as a whole accumulated 865.5 hours towards their abatements.
  19| Eligibility Requirements:
  20|  Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  21|  Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
  22|  Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse’s name who is also 60 years of age or
  23| older
  24|  Meet the income guidelines of :
  25| One person household - $40,000 / Two person household - $60,000
  26|  All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re- apply and be
  27| accepted annually for participation in the program.
  28| Earnings are exempt from state taxes only.  Earnings are subject to federal and FICA tax withholdings
  29| and are also subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional
  30| Retirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.
  31| Departments who participated between January 1, 2015 – November 1, 2015:
  32| Lunenburg Board of Health
  33| Lunenburg Building Department
  34| Lunenburg Council on Aging
  35| Lunenburg DPW/Sewer Departments
  36| Lunenburg Police Dept.
  37| Lunenburg Public Library
  38| Lunenburg School Department

===PAGE 70=== (plain, 48 lines)
   1| 70
   2| SEWER COMMISSION
   3| The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006.
   4| At the first Sewer Commission meeting after the May 2014 Town election, when the Commission elects the
   5| officers for  the next year, Carl Luck was elected to remain as Chairman, Dave MacDonald to serve as Vice-
   6| Chair and Mike Nault to serve as Clerk.  Other members on the Commission were Butch Bilotta and Troy
   7| Daniels, who was elected to the Sewer Commission in the May 2014 Town Election as a write-in candidate to
   8| fill the vacancy left by Jay Simeone who did not run for re-election. The Sewer Commission bears the
   9| responsibility of overseeing the Town’s sewer system, planning for the future, and developing and implementing
  10| rules, regulations and policies.  The Department of Public Works manages and maintains the operational side of
  11| the system.  Under the Town’s Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and maintenance of the
  12| sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager.   The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a
  13| sewer treatment plant. Approximately 81% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the
  14| other 19% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
  15| There are currently 764 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 378 that have access to Town
  16| Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY15, 36 new accounts connected to Town Sewer.  The Commission
  17| billed out $715,400 in quarterly sewer bills, and had $41,759 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners’
  18| property tax bills for non-payment.   Nine properties with access to sewer were assessed privilege fees that had
  19| not originally been assessed betterments.  Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by the Town on the
  20| original sewer project.
  21| In FY15, it was required by the State Department of Revenue that the Sewer Enterprise and the Sewer
  22| Betterment Funds be combined for a consolidated statement, and for the first time, the Commission had to
  23| project the year-to-year income over expense as pertains to betterment income versus debt service.  With these
  24| revisions to the budget, it was necessary to request a transfer from Retained Earnings for $77,401.80 (Article 17,
  25| May 2015 Town Meeting) to cover both general expenses and debt service for the next fiscal year.  The goal of
  26| the Sewer Commission is to maintain a balanced budget going forward and to build up the Retained Earnings so
  27| that it may be used for major capital projects to maintain and improve the operating efficiency of the system.
  28| In August 2013, the Sewer Commission began contracting with Small Water Systems Services of Littleton
  29| (SWSS) for the Operations and Management of the 10 pumping stations and sewer infrastructure. The Sewer
  30| Commission has been very satisfied with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with SWSS, renewing the
  31| contract again for FY15.  Since the start of the contract, additional responsibilities have been added to the SWSS
  32| contract, including installing and monitoring the SCADA system at the Leominster Road station in March 2015
  33| and taking on the maintenance of the 10 generators housed in our pumping stations.
  34| The Sewer Commission continued work on preparing for the major sewer project in the Pratt Street/Lakeview
  35| Ave sections of town, using the State Revolving Fund (SRF) program throughout FY15, to connect 80 plus
  36| homes in areas of need as determined by the Comprehensive Wastewater Management Plan to public sewer.
  37| Forty-two of these homes were in close proximity to Whalom Lake and were of concern to the homeowners,
  38| Board of Health and environmentalists.  The proposed sewer extensions had attained a satisfactory rating by the
  39| State to be assured of an SRF loan and the Commission presented the project at the spring Town Meeting in
  40| May 2014, obtaining approval to move the project forward.  The Commission worked continuously with the
  41| engineering firm, Wright Pierce, throughout the year on design and engineering.  In the spring of 2015, the
  42| Commission put the project out to bid and in May 2015 awarded the construction bid to CJP Construction out of
  43| Millis, MA, with construction to commence in the summer of 2015.
  44| Also in the spring of 2015, the Commission, working with Weston & Sampson Engineering, continued their
  45| work to find sources of I/I (Inflow/Infiltration) by performing 171 house-to-house inspections in the Mass
  46| Ave./Leominster Rd. areas in the spring of 2015.  These efforts were funded by the Sewer Bank Fee, established
  47| in 2013 as required by the Inter-Municipal Agreement with the City of Leominster, collecting $220. per
  48| bedroom on new connections.  Weston & Sampson worked to ensure proper connections from these 171

===PAGE 71=== (plain, 39 lines)
   1| 71
   2| locations to the public sewer and advising those property owners when an illegal connection was discovered,
   3| whether it is from a sump pump in the cellar or roof drain.  These sources only increase the town’s expenses as
   4| they are sent via public sewer pipes to the wastewater treatment plants in either Leominster or Fitchburg,
   5| increasing their flows and capacities unnecessarily.
   6| The Commission has continued to work hard to enforce the Grease Trap Regulation for all Food Service
   7| Establishments that have or should have a grease trap or grease interceptor.   Annual permit fees were assessed
   8| in July 2015 based on the establishment’s level of compliance, and the reporting requirements clearly
   9| communicated.  Lunenburg has 19 grease trap establishments.  This regulation will continue to be strongly
  10| enforced as fats, oils and grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the
  11| sewer system.  While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal, individual home
  12| owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increases everyone’s costs. The
  13| Commission understands that education is an important aspect of their role and will continue to provide
  14| information to their customers to keep the sewer system running economically and efficiently by reducing the
  15| disposal of fats, oil and grease into the system to avoid blockages. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not
  16| be put down the drain.  Another enemy of the sewer system is “disposable wipes” which do not biodegrade and
  17| bring our pumps to a halt.  Nothing but organic waste and toilet paper should be put into the sewer system.
  18| During FY15 and into FY16, the Commission continued working on several projects, with Commissioners
  19| serving as Project Champions.  They are:
  20|  Lakeview/Pratt extension
  21|  I/I reduction project
  22|  Grease trap compliance
  23|  Flow Rate/Metering
  24|  Privately Funded Extension Policy update
  25|  Business Systems and Process Improvement
  26| The Commission would like to thank Dave MacDonald and Butch Bilotta, who both stepped off the
  27| Commission in May 2015, for all their hard work and years of dedication and service.  In the town election of
  28| May 2015, the Commission was happy to welcome Sue Bunish to the Commission, and John Reynolds who was
  29| appointed to the Commission in July 2015. Troy Daniels stepped off the Commission in January 2016.  Bob
  30| Ebersole continues to serve as the Board of Selectmen liaison and the Commission is grateful for his knowledge
  31| and counsel.  Jay Simeone, past member of the Sewer Commission, serves as the Finance Committee liaison to
  32| blend his experience on the Commission with its financial planning and we thank him for his participation. The
  33| Commission would also like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director, for the seamless and smooth operation of
  34| the Sewer division of the DPW and also Jim Breault (DPW-Facilities) who whenever called upon, provides his
  35| expertise and service to the Sewer Department.
  36| Carl Luck, Chair
  37| Mike Nault, Vice Chair
  38| Sue Bunish, Member
  39| John Reynolds, Member

   2| BOARD OF HEALTH
   3| The Board of Health office is located in  the Ritter Library B uilding on the corner of Mass. Ave and
   4| Leominster Road.  Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.  We can be reached at
   5| 978-345-4146, extension 430.
   6| The Board of Health with the assistance of its a gent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health , continued
   7| its mission to maintain its focus on public heal th and safety issues during 201 5, by reviewing Title V
   8| inspection reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations , housing inspections,
   9| complaints and general public health related issues.
  10| This past year has seen the reinstatement of the Septic System Repair Program which helps low to
  11| moderate income families replace fail ed septic systems or connect to public sewer.  Anyone interested in
  12| finding out more about this program can contact the Board of Health office.
  13| Homeland Security planning is ongoing.  Meetings continue between our agents, other Town Departments
  14| and region al entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but also with our partner  towns in our
  15| region.
  16| This year we processed applications for septic permit lot t ests, permits for new construction,  new wells,
  17| repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits.  The Board continues its vigilance with regard to
  18| septic systems, wells, food service inspection s, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to
  19| protect the environment and groundwaters of the Commonwealth.  The Board of Health is als o represented
  20| on the recently created Stormwater Task Force.
  21| The Board of Health  provides numerous other services in cooperation with our health agents, the Nashoba
  22| Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental  services, n umerous clinics a re provided
  23| throughout the year to our residents.  These clinics provide inoculations, blood pressure screenings, Well
  24| Oldster Clinics and d ental screening. Fluoride treatment and dental checks of our youngsters in the school
  25| system are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
  26| The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project.  Mosquito Control
  27| monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as EEE and
  28| West Nile Virus . It  sprays frequentl y to control the population during the warmer times of the year.
  29| Residents who want to have their property spr ayed can do so by emailing to : cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling
  30| (508) 393-3055, completing a service request on their webpa ge at:  http:// www.cmmcp.org or  calling the
  31| Board of Health office.
  32| Along with our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners, we provide in-home visits to residents recovering
  33| from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  34| Please remember that our Board m embers volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the c itizens of
  35| Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the
  36| Board of Health office at 582-4146 ext. 430.

===PAGE 76=== (layout, 24 lines)
   1|                                       NASHOBA ASSOCIATED BOARDS OF HEALTH
   2| Nashoba Associated Boards of Health continues to serve the local Board of Health in meeting the
   3| challenges of environmental and public health needs in Lunenburg.  In addition to the day to day
   4|  public health work conducted for Lunenburg we also provide the following services.
   5|     Maintaining Nashoba’s Internet web site to provide information for the public. (See nashoba.org)

   7|      ance of the necessary permits.
   8|     Response to state mandated changes in regulations requiring more frequent beach sampling through sample
   9|      collection, submittal for analysis, and follow-up when results were obtained.

   5|                                 The Nashoba sanitarian is generally scheduled to be available for the public twice a
   6|                              week on Monday and Wednesday mornings at the Lunenburg Board of Health Office.
   7|                              Other meetings occur informally.
   8|                          This does not reflect the daily calls handled by the three Nashoba secretaries during
   9|                              daily business hours.
  10|                          Food Service Licenses & Inspections.....................………49
  11|                          Nashoba annually mails out and receives application from both restaurants and retail food
  12|                          businesses.  Licenses are renewed for existing facilities.  Plans are submitted and reviewed
  13|                          prior to initial licensing. Most licensees are inspected at a minimum twice a year. Where
  14|                          deficiencies are found, a re-inspection is scheduled to insure compliance.  When a com-
  15|                          plaint from the public is received an inspection is also conducted. During this inspection
  16|                          health factors of food handlers is also investigated, and where appropriate medical consul-
  17|                          tation and laboratory testing may be required.
  18|                          Beach/Camp Inspections.................................................. 23
  19|                          Camps are inspected at least annually at opening for compliance with State Sanitary Code,
  20|                          Chapter IV, 105 CMR. 430.00. Public swimming beaches are sampled for water quality every
  21|                          week during the summer and more often if a problem is suspected.
  22|                          Housing & Nuisance Investigations...................................38
  23|                          Nashoba, as agent for the local Board of Health, inspects dwellings for conformance with
  24|                          State Sanitary Code, 105 CMR 410.00, issues orders for correction, and re-inspect for com-
  25|                          pliance.  Similarly, complaints from residents concerning unsanitary conditions or pollution
  26|                          events are investigated.
  27|                          Septic System Test Applications.......................................78
  28|                          Applications from residents proposing to build or upgrade a septic system are accepted, a file
  29|                          created, and testing dates are coordinated with the applicants engineer.
  30|                          Septic System Lot Tests........................................234
  31|                             Nashoba sanitarian witnesses soil evaluations, deep hole tests, and, usually on a separate
  32|                          date, percolation tests conducted by the applicant’s engineer which serve as the basis of
  33|                          the design of the septic system.

  28| public on a variety of health and safety issues (immunizations, head lice, chronic disease
  29| management, safe drug/sharps disposal etc.) in a variety of ways.

  31| to all, especially the underserved and at-risk populations.

  33| guidelines and strategies that protect the hea lth of individual and the community at
  34| large (mandated by the Massachusetts Department of Public Health).
