   1| 81
   2|                                              FIRE DEPARTMENT
   3|                                                       655 Massachusetts Ave
   4|        CHIEF
   5|  Patrick A. Sullivan
   6|  TEL: (978) 582-4155
   7|  FAX: (978) 582-4033
   8| The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to
   9| providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the
  10| Town of Lunenburg.
  11| In 2015 your Fire Department responded to 1985 calls for service, broken down as follows:
  12| Fires & Hazardous Conditions      483                Emergency Medical      967
  13| Inspections    493                                             Other                         37
  14|      Fire Department staffing today is five Career Staff and forty to forty-five On Call Firefighters and Emergency
  15| Medical Technicians.  The station is staffed 7 days per week from 6:00 a.m. - 6:00 p.m.  The career staff man the
  16| station Monday through Friday.  On Saturday and Sunday the station is manned by two of our On Call
  17| Firefighter/EMTs on an hourly “per diem” basis.  At night however, all of our personnel respond from home on
  18| an “on call” basis.   This adds several minutes or more to our response time as personnel must respond to the
  19| station, pick up the appropriate equipment and then respond the call.
  20|      The addition of the Weekend Day coverage began on July 1, 2015 and has significantly decreased response
  21| times during the time the station is manned.  This is the first significant change to our staffing in nearly 15 years,
  22| even though we have seen an almost 48% increase in our call volume during that same time period.  We still rely
  23| on our On Call Personnel to be the primary responders 50% of the week.
  24|      2015 was our busiest year to date, with a 1.5% overall increase in activity from 2014 and a 5.5% increase in
  25| EMS related calls.  As our community continues to grow I can only expect the demand for emergency services to
  26| grow along with it.  While our Call Force continues to do an amazing job, the ever increasing call volume is
  27| straining our ability to provide the high level of service townspeople have come to expect.  This leads me to
  28| continue to call for 24 hour staffing in the station.  Funding to maintain 2 Firefighter/EMT’s in the station 24
  29| hours a day, 7 days a week would allow either the first ambulance or first piece of fire apparatus to respond
  30| immediately.  Not only would this reduce our response times to critical emergencies, it would also reduce the
  31| “wear and tear” on our Call Staff.   This coverage would in no way reduce the need for our On Call personnel; it
  32| would only supplement their response and reduce response times.
  33|       Our firefighters continue to train for the myriad of situations which can be encountered even in a town such as
  34| ours.  Today’s firefighter must be skilled in not only firefighting, but medical care, hazardous materials mitigation
  35| and technical rescue.  Our EMT’s must continue to stay abreast of the latest treatment protocols and advances in
  36| lifesaving skills.  Personnel train regularly both here at the station and at classes sponsored by other agencies.
  37| While they are compensated for some of the training, the vast majority is done on their own time.

===PAGE 82=== (plain, 48 lines)
   1| 82
   2| As an example, a new Firefighter/EMT must attend ove r 340 hours of basic firefighting and Emergen cy
   3| Medical Technician training.  Once they are certif ied, they must complete over 4 0 hours of continuing education
   4| and refresher training every two years, just for their EMT.  Operators of Fire Apparatus must complete an
   5| additional 65 hours of training in Driver training, pump and aerial operation and hydraulics calculations.
   6| Several of our  personnel are members of Regional Technical Rescue units, providing services for
   7| Confined Space and Cave in Rescue, as well as Underwater Search and Recovery.  They stand ready to respond as
   8| needed throughout Northern Worcester County.
   9| 2015 continued to be an active year for our Fire Prevention efforts.   By law we are required to inspect
  10| smoke and carbon monoxide detector installations when a home is sold, as well as  all new oil burner and propane
  11| gas installations.  A ny business hol ding a liquor license is also required to be inspected  for fire safety issues. In
  12| addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as well  as
  13| ongoing inspections during the construction process.   Personnel also continue to provide fire safety education to
  14| not only children in our schools but adults as well.
  15| Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2015 include:
  16| - Continuation of a D epartment of Fire Se rvices Grant in the amount of $6953 . to assist with Fir e
  17| Safety Education in both the schools and with senior c itizens.  This grant provides funding for both
  18| materials and personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations.  Highlights
  19| of activity through this grant include:
  20| o Educational programs in basic fire safety in Grades K -3, including use of the Regional Fire
  21| Safety Trailer.
  22| o Educational programs in conjunction with the Council on Aging.  These included co oking
  23| and heating safety, a CPR Demonstration and ice safety.
  24| - Participation in the Senior Center’s “Sand for Seniors” Program.  This program provid es buckets of
  25| walkway sand for senior c itizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter mont hs.
  26| In partnership with this program, our Fire Prevention Officer Captain James Ricci delivers the sand to
  27| the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for smoke and carbon monoxide d etectors.  If
  28| the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci w ould install free detectors obtained through the
  29| Senior SAFE Grant.
  30| - The Department also partnered with the American Red Cross to install free smoke detectors  in
  31| sections of town.  Combined with the “Sand of Seniors” program, 93 homes were visited and over 70
  32| smoke and carbon monoxide detectors were installed.
  33| - It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent
  34| smoke detectors.
  35| Several members are also Certified First Aid and CPR Instructors and provide training to both citizens
  36| and town employees.
  37| Our d epartment continues to actively pursue grant f unding opportunities as they occur, though those
  38| opportunities are becoming scarcer.
  39| The new Engine 1 , approved in the FY ’15 Capital Plan was placed in se rvice in July of 2015 and is
  40| providing outstanding service as our primary pumper.  It replaced a 1991 model.
  41| Apparatus replacement continues to be a concern.  FY’15 saw a significant increase in vehicle maintenance costs,
  42| particularly with the older apparatus.  The timely replacement of our older apparatus will hopefully decrease some
  43| of those costs.  Two of our front line units (Engine 4 and Ladder 1) are over 20 years old and showing their age
  44| through increasing maintenance costs and “out of service” time.  Engine 4 in particular has seen over $50,000 in
  45| repairs over the last 3 years, and has averaged a month per year out of service.  The new Engine 1 is handing a
  46| higher volume of responses to decrease the wear and tear on Engine 4, hopefully prolonging its lifespan.  These
  47| units are included the department’s 5 Year Capital Plan for replacement in FY ’17 and FY ’18 respectively and it
  48| is hoped that due consideration will be given for their timely replacement.

  36| provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in today’s challenging environments.   All newly hired

  42| education.  It also provides the department with additional assistance as these members cover some On Call shifts
  43| from the station.

  46| would also like to thank the other department heads, board s and commissions for their continuing help.  Most
  47| people do not realize the level of interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business.
  48| My thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing support of public safety .  It is that support
  49| that allows us to continue providing the services you expect from us.

  25| to the community embodied the principles of commitment and service.  We wish Paul the same success
  26| and happiness in retirement that he experienced during his time here.
  27| Linda Carrier retired after 32 years as a Reserve Police Officer, and sexual assault investigator. Linda was
  28| first employed by the Town in 1981 and in 1982 accepted a position with the police department as the
  29| executive assistant to the Chief of Police. In 1984 Linda was appointed as a Reserve Police officer and
  30| subsequently certified as a sexual assault investigator. She was later promoted to public safety
  31| coordinator, a position she still maintains today. We commend Linda for her ability to command two
  32| converging professions within the police department for more than 30 years. It saddens us that we’ll no
  33| longer find Linda in a police uniform, but we take solace in that we know she’s still part of the support
  34| staff.

   4| Animal Control
   5| Animal Control has recently partnered with the Police Department. Kathleen Comeau is and will remain
   6| the Director of Animal Control. As a partnership Kathleen will be able to utilize the Police Records
   7| management system to enhance her ability to maintain and record animal complaints,
   8| lost/missing/abducted and abuse investigations as well as licensing, and kenneling issues. The public is
   9| asked to call in any animal control related complaints to the Regional Call Center, Nashoba Valley
  10| Regional Dispatch, at (978) 582-4531. The Animal Control Web-Site, lunenburgma.gov. Remember,
  11| dogs must be licensed by April 1st annually. Dogs must have a current Rabies vaccination certificate to
  12| qualify for licensing. This can be done at the Town Clerk's office in Town Hall, 17 Main Street.
  13| Unspayed Female/Unaltered Male $20 fee. Spayed Female/Altered Male $5 fee.  Late Fee: $25.
  14| State of the Police Department
  15| The Police Department is in operation 24 hours a day 7 days a week.  Two patrol officers are assigned to
  16| each shift with support from the Traffic Enforcement/K-9 Officer and the Community Outreach Officer
  17| who each work two day shifts and two evening shifts.  A Reserve Officer is assigned to the desk on a full
  18| time basis from 7:00 a.m. to 3:00 p.m. Monday through Friday. The desk is extended from 3:00 p.m. to
  19| 7:00 p.m. during the week on a per-diem basis.
  20| The Police were dispatched to approximately 5600 calls for service and self-initiated another 7800 calls,
  21| responded to 278 motor vehicle accidents, investigated 1253 incidents, conducted 4039 motor vehicle
  22| stops, wrote 2440 motor vehicle citations, arrested 371 people, summonsed 259, and placed 12 people in
  23| protective custody.
  24| In addition to the calls dispatched by the regional call center, the desk officer answered some 7000 phone
  25| calls, took approximately 3200 lobby inquiries and complaints, and wrote, roughly, 153 reports in 2015.
  26| This year the desk watch officer was responsible for the suicide watch of 146 detainees either taken into
  27| custody overnight or during the daytime hours.
  28| In the past five years the police department has realized a 180.1 percent increase in arrests, a 61.9%
  29| increase in reportable incidents, incidents that require a follow-up investigation and/ or court complaint,
  30| and a 20% increase in motor vehicle accidents. I don’t anticipate that these figures are going to decline as
  31| the Town continues to grow and the level of traffic in and around Lunenburg increases.

  63| This is a comparison of towns with similar demographics and similar bordering communities as
  64| well as a few communities that border Lunenburg:
  65| The workload has once again fueled the request for additional Police Officers... The work load will
  66| exceed 23,000 hours this year. With 14 officers, including the Police Chief, staffing deployment would
  67| have to be adjusted over 100 times to effectively allocate resources and meet daily needs. The FBI
  68| Uniform Crime Report, Table 71 indicates that the average number of police officers in a New England
  69| town with a population between 10,000 and 24, 999 should be 1.8 per 1000 inhabitants. We are presently
  70| at 1.4 per 1000 inhabitants.
  71| In this comparison Lunenburg has the highest number of arrests and incidents with the lowest number of
  72| officer employed. The range is from 17 officers to 22 officers.
  73| Request for staffing is an old story. The Police Department has been asking for a staff increase for over 20
  74| years. Here are some statements taken from previous town reports as far back as 1996:
  75| 1996 12 Officers  As a result of obtaining a COPS MORE grant from the Federal Government in the
  76| amount of 75,000, we were able to hire on additional police officer, bringing our police force up to twelve
  77| (12) full time officers.
  78| 2004 13 officers:  Low staffing levels continue to be an issue for our Police Department. The number of
  79| full-time officers is significantly lower than recommended levels for a community our size.  As our
  80| community grows, and as the communities around grow, so do the number of calls for service.

   6| number of full time officers (12).

  41| apprehension, placing the safety and security of others before our own with honor.

  45| above the law and always inspiring the confidence of our community whether on or off duty.

   2| CAPITAL PLANNING COMMITTEE
   3| Lunenburg’s Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year (FY)
   4| Plan and a Capital Improvement Plan. These Plans are to be presented to the Town Manager.
   5| As noted in the past reports, the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety, Need, Crisis,
   6| Cost Effectiveness and Ability to Fund. The Committee has to review each request under these six
   7| elements. Not only do they have to determine that the request fits within the criteria, but they have to
   8| prioritize the level that meets the criteria. Therefore, not all requests are included in the FY Plans, mostly
   9| due to funding. The result of the proposed FY Plans provides the needed service for safety and protection
  10| of the public, the upkeep to our schools and the management of government.
  11| At the Annual Town Meeting May 2015, the following items, totaling $ 1,051,578, were presented in the
  12| FY16 Plan.
  13| The Committee meets each year after the Capital Budget is presented to the Town Manager and
  14| considers improvement to the methodology of creating the budget. In future preparation the
  15| Committee will conduct more meetings on site.

   2| PLANNING BOARD
   3| FY2014-2015
   4| The Planning Board welcomed new member s David Prokowiew and Gregory Bittner.   Joanna Bilotta-Simeone
   5| and Nathan Lockwood departed from the Board after serving a number of years.
   6| Six ANR plans (subdivision of lots for separate building lots)  were approved. Approved residential subdivisions
   7| were in various stages of completion .  The following continued to build:  Emerald Place at Lake Whalom, Stone
   8| Farm Estates  off Massachusetts Avenue , Seq uoia Drive off Howard Street, and a nine -lot subdivision at 50
   9| Elmwood Road.
  10| Tri-Town Landing  off Youngs Road  began construction on Building 4  with anticipation of spring 2016
  11| occupancy.  Highfield V illage off Northfield Road was in review .  Whites Woods Phase III was built to
  12| completion.
  13| Whitetail Crossing  off Burrage Street  received Town acceptance of the roadway interior at May 2015 Town
  14| Meeting.
  15| The Board completed site plan reviews for construction of a combined middle/high school, a landscaping facility
  16| on Massachusetts Avenue, and a consignment shop on Massachusetts Avenue.
  17| With the assistance of MRPC , a public forum and a walking tour  of the Center were held in August; this would
  18| assist the Board in its creation of the Village Center District Bylaw.  Numerous public workshops were held and
  19| the Planning Board and the Historical Commission spent many months working together on the creation of a
  20| Village Center District Zoning Bylaw and an Architectural Preservation District General Bylaw , both of which
  21| passed at Town Meeting.  The creation of the Village Center District is intended to encourage growth that creates
  22| a dynamic active environment while retaining the area's existing character.   The intent of the  Architectural
  23| Preservation District Bylaw will be to protect historically and/or architecturally significant buildings.
  24| 790-792 Massachusetts Avenue was rezoned from Commercial to Limited Business/Residential.  The rezoning of
  25| this site  (well known as Doc’s), placed these two parcels into the surrounding zoning.  Also of note was the
  26| residents’ voting at Town Meeting to designate both Flat Hill Road and Northfield Road as scenic roads.
  27| The Town received approval from the Department of Housing & Community Development for its Housing
  28| Production Plan .  This p lan will aid Lunenburg in guiding housing development and can influence the type,
  29| amount, and location of affordable housing.
  30| The meeting room in the Ritter Memorial Building was named in honor of Marion M. Benson for her many years
  31| of service to the Town.
  32| Board members served as representatives to the following committees:  Joanna Bilotta-Simeone, Capital Planning
  33| Committee and Montac husett Regional Planning Commission; Nathan Lockwood, School Building Committee;
  34| Damon McQuaid, Building ReUse C ommittee; Kenneth Chenis, Montachusett  Joint Transportation Committee ;
  35| and Matthew Allison, liaison to the Agricultural Commission.
  36| The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be reached at
  37| 978-582-4147 extension 5.  Various forms are available online at http://lunenburgma.gov/  on the Planning
  38| Board’s homepage.  The Zoning Map, Protective Bylaws for the Town, and the Rules and Regulations Governing
  39| the Subdivision of Land are available online.

   1| 95
   2| ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   3| The Zoning Board of Appeals conducted fourteen hearings during calendar year 2015, all for special permits. Thirteen of the
   4| requested special permits were granted and one was denied. There were no applications for variances.
   5| The Zoning Board meets on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month on an "as needed" basis. The members are:
   6| Raymond E. Beal, Chairman, Donald F. Bowen, Vice Chairman, James Besarkarski, Alfred Gravelle, Hans Wentrup and
   7| associate members, David Blatt, Paul Doherty and Sheila Lumi. Lisa Normandin is the Administrative Assistant to the
   8| Zoning Board.
   9| The functions of the Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statutes are as follows:
  10| 1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from the Town's Protective Bylaws as authorized in
  11| the Bylaws.
  12| 2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective Bylaws.
  13| 3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts
  14| General Laws, as amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain
  15| a permit or enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the
  16| Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official.
  17| 4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General
  18| Laws, Chapter 40B. The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits
  19| submitted to the Board under Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.
  20| These requests can by law, circumvent local zoning ordinances.
  21| The Office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
  22| Avenue. If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals, please contact Lisa Normandin at 978-582-4146 X
  23| 421 during regular business hours. Zoning Board applications (with instructions) are available from the office.

   2| Superintendent’s Message
   3| What a joy it has been to participate in the construction of the middle -high school.   Every Wednesday after the construction
   4| meeting, I had the opportunity to tour the site and building with our project manag er and architect.  A year ago that meant
   5| walking around large concrete pads and steel beams.  Now it is walking through a high school classroom wing that will be
   6| complete within days including the installation of student lockers in the hallways.  As of Feb ruary 19, 2016, the school
   7| building construction is over 80% complete with 70 to 80 workers on site on any given work day.  Occupancy C ertificate
   8| receipt is expected to be on hand late May 2016.  Furniture delive ries and the move are scheduled for May and June 2016.

  10| need for the temporary heating equipment. Heating through the fin tube radiation is in place in all of the building. By the end
  11| of this month all temporary electrical systems in the building will be removed with permanent lighting and power operational
  12| throughout. The elevator installations are complete with the exception of call button and lanterns installation.  The
  13| installation of interior glass in hollow metal frames is underway with areas “A” and “D” complete as of mid -February 2016.

===PAGE 101=== (plain, 53 lines)
   1| 101
   2| The building project has its own website at our district website www.lunenburgschools.net and Facebook page -
   3| Lunenburg Middle/High School Building Project.  The District will return to a four school configuration with the
   4| opening of the new co -located middle/high school in August, 2016.  Each building will have a smaller student
   5| population than the current configuration.   The Primary School will serve students PK -2.  Grades 3-5 will be at
   6| Turkey Hill Elementary School.  Grades 6-12 will be in the new co-located middle/high school.
   7| The new school was designed to operate as two separate educational programs.  The new building has a classroom
   8| wing for the middle school an d another classroom wing for high school.   The 6th grade students will be located
   9| on the first floor of the middle school wing.  Classrooms for grades 7 and 8 are located on the second floor of the
  10| middle school wing. Middle school and high school student s will enter the building via separate entrances.
  11| Located behind the classroom wings are the cafeteria, gym, auditorium, central offices, health services, and
  12| library/media center.  There are an appropriate number of art, music, and technology classrooms for both middle
  13| and high school programs which are clustered together in the central core of the building near the auditorium.   On
  14| the second floor above the school offices there are foreign language classrooms. The gymnasium can be divided at
  15| center court by a curtain to accommodate two large physical education classes.  There is also a weight room and
  16| an alternate physical education room with a substantial dance/yoga area, exercise bikes and treadmills.  There are
  17| two locker rooms for the middle school s tudents and two locker rooms for the high school students.  A health
  18| education classroom is also located near the gym.
  19| Lauren Drury Basque is the 2015 Superintendent's Award recipient.  She is the daughter of Kenneth and Suzanne
  20| Basque.  At LHS Lauren focused her studies on the sciences including numerous honors and advanced placement
  21| courses.  She is ranked first in her class.  Lauren was honored at the Worcester County Superintendent's
  22| Roundtable Scholars' ceremony in January.  She selected a quote f rom Eleanor Roosevelt for that ceremony's
  23| program.  The quote certainly captures the essence of this remarkable young woman.  "The purpose of life is to
  24| live it, to taste experience to the utmost, to reach out eagerly and without fear for newer and richer experience."
  25| Ms. Basque personifies our mission for lifelong learning and responsible community membership; constantly
  26| learning, growing, achieving and giving.  She inspires us with her joy of learning and service to her community.

   2| Lunenburg students continue to excel both in and outside the classroom.  Our students continue to perform well
   3| on state and national assessments.  They distinguish themselves with their various talents.  Our athletic teams
   4| competed successfully at the district and state levels.   Our student musicians performed at S ymphony Hall with
   5| the Handel and Haydn Society Period Orchestra.  School thespians entertained and overwhelmed us with their
   6| performances at the fiftieth annual competitive class plays.  The marching band captivated us with their half -time
   7| show again this fall, and the middle and high school music concerts have been most enjoyable and uplifting.
   8| Lunenburg students’ work in the visual arts was exhibited locally and at state competitions.
   9| This past year our schools bid farewell to retirees who devoted many years to public service.  Retiring this past
  10| year were Suzanne Saven, School Psychologist; Mary Ann Weisman, Primary School Special Education Teacher;
  11| Kristin Mackay, School Social Worker;  Annette Kelly, Cafeteria Monitor; Joanne Deming, THMS Teacher;
  12| Marlene Wiita,  Paraprofessional; Vivian Butler, Paraprofessional; Sandra Laserte, THMS Physical Education
  13| Teacher; Sharon Kimball, LHS Foreign Language Teacher; and Phil McMurray,  Guidance Counselor.    We
  14| appreciate the many contributions of these dedicat ed and committed professionals and extend our best wishes to
  15| them in their retirement.
  16| I am grateful for the large number of positive role models who serve in our community.  It is a significant strength
  17| for which Lunenburg can be proud.  Our children and youth have available to them many caring adults who serve
  18| others every day.  They share their time and talents for the greater good.  Students, parents, community
  19| volunteers, elected officials, town personnel, and civic organizations are actively engaged in addressing the
  20| challenges we confronted locally and beyond.  We are a community of doers and our school curriculum reflects
  21| that action-oriented, problem-solving approach.  The faculty, staff, and students of the Lunenburg Public Schools
  22| share with the community this tradition of promoting service to others.
  23| Respectfully Submitted,
  24| Loxi Jo Calmes, Superintendent of Schools
  25| School Facilities
  26| 2015-2016
  27| Construction of the new Middle School/High School continued throughout the year with completion of the
  28| building expected by late May 2016. Shawmut Construction Management continues to execute the building
  29| project with NV5, having acquired Joslin, Lesser, & Associates, providing owner’s project management services.
  30| The School Building Committee continues to provide construction and financial oversight. The exterior envelope
  31| was completed by the fall of 2015 which allowed for interior finish work to continue throughout the following
  32| winter. The project continued on time and budget despite the challenges of substantial snowfall in February 2015.
  33| Phase 2, which includes construction of the new greenhouse, removal of asbestos in the high school as well as
  34| demolition, construction of two athletic fields, modifying the student parking lot, tennis courts, cons truction of an
  35| outdoor basketball court and remaining site work will commence in June 2016. Phase 2 is expected to be
  36| complete by late December 2016.
  37| The FY 16 capital plan approved funding of the district technology plan at $36,200. The FY 15 capital pla n
  38| funded $138,000 for asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill Middle School which had been unable to be  completed
  39| during the summer of 2014. In July 2015, four classrooms had abatement work (removal of tile ceilings,
  40| abatement of spray-on fireproofing and vinyl asbestos tiles) completed and returned to a usable condition for the
  41| school year at a cost of $119,803. Montachusett  Contracting Company Inc. completed the work by August 1,
  42| 2015. Unexpended funds are expected to be utilized to continue asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill Middle School.
  43| The focus of school maintenance for FY16 continues to be preventative maintenance of heating,
  44| ventilation systems and energy conservation measures particularly of the schools that will operate in FY
  45| 17. Conversion of existing lighting to LED remains a high priority for Lunenburg Primary School and
  46| Turkey Hill Middle School. Preparations for the reorganization of the district in June 2016 were also a
  47| focus area throughout the year. Identifying materials that need to move as part of the reorganization and
  48| determining the disposition of furniture and equipment that will be surplus once the new school building
  49| opens has been ongoing since July 2015. This

===PAGE 103=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 103
   2| will culminate with a contractor move of teaching supplies to the new building  in June 2016 and the removal of
   3| all non-fixed items from the current high school by the end of June 2016.
   4| During the year, we have continued to fine tune plans for two LED conversion projects using Green Community
   5| Funds. The project for Turkey Hill Middl e School is to convert all 28 Watt florescent lamps to 18 Watt LED
   6| lamps at a cost of $36,253 with a resultant 6.7 year payback in energy savings. At Lunenburg Primary School,
   7| conversion of high bay fixtures in the gym and media center to LED are also plan ned as well as conversion of 28
   8| Watt fluorescent lamps at a cost of $44,145 with a 6.4 year pay back.
   9| For the FY 17 Capital Program, the School Department has requested an additional $145,000 in funding for
  10| continued asbestos removal at Turkey Hill as its highest priority.  Two unfunded
  11| FY 16 requests to replace the Turkey Hill phone system and intercom system at $22,000 each were priorities 2
  12| and 3. The school department request to fund HVAC upgrades for the district office relocation to Turkey Hill
  13| Middle school was withdrawn following a decision to hold district offices at the Passios Elementary School until
  14| the town is prepared to have the Passios undergo renovations to become offices for town government.
  15| Respectfully submitted,
  16| John Londa, Director of Facilities and Grounds
  17| Special Services Department
  18| FY15 (data taken from 2014-2015 school year)
  19| Julianna Hanscom, Director of Special Services
  20| From the March 1, 2015 report, there are two hundred and seventy students with disabilities receiving
  21| special education or related services in Lunenburg.  This represents 17.2% of our total student population of 1,567
  22| students (it was 17.8% last year).  The state average from the 2015 school report card is 17.1%.  As of March 1,
  23| 2015, the total number of studen ts receiving services outside the district was thirty.  Of the thirty students, nine
  24| are placed in Collaborative Placements, fifteen are in Day Placements and six are in Residential Placements.
  25| Additionally, one hundred sixteen students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in
  26| Lunenburg.
  27| This year as a district, our Accountability and Assistance Level is Level 2 with Turkey Hill Middle
  28| School listed as Level 2 and Lunenburg High School listed as Level 1 (LHS was Level 2 last year).  The state
  29| report card lists Students with Disabilities in Lunenburg as “Did Not Meet Target”.  The district’s Students with
  30| Disabilities progress towards narrowing proficiency gaps [Cumulative Progress and Performance Index (CPI): 1-
  31| 100)] is listed at 46 (it was 50 last year, and 54 the year prior) with the target set for 75.  Students with Disabilities
  32| participated in MCAS this year with the following rates:  English Language Arts = 99% (it was 97% last year),
  33| Mathematics = 99% (It was 98% last year), and Science = 100% (It was 96% last year).  This data can be found at
  34| the DESE website:
  35| http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/accountability/report/district.aspx?linkid=30&orgcode=01620000&orgtypecode=5&

  38| Indicators, the following information is available below and also can be found at:
  39| http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped_map.aspx?orgcode=01620000&

  42| Indicator 2 – Dropout Rate.  For the 2013 -2014 school year, the state target for Students with Disabilities was
  43| 3.3%. Lunenburg rate was 2.2% and the overall state rate was 2.1% for Students with Disabilities.

  19| Indicator 4 – Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs.  For Lunenburg, our rate was
  20| only .7%.  The state rate was 1.2%.   Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant discrepanc ies with the
  21| Suspension/Expulsion rates by race or ethnicity.  In 2014, Lunenburg was not found to have any significant
  22| discrepancies due to these factors.
  23| Indicator 5 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 6-21 with IEPs. For 2013-2014, the state’s target rates
  24| are as follows:  for Full Inclusion of students = 60.5%, Partial Inclusion = “not identified”, Substantially Separate
  25| Placements = 14.5%, and for Separate Schools/Residential Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 5.5%.  In
  26| Lunenburg, our percentages are as follows:  Full Inclusion = 73.8, Partial = 8.9%, Substantially Separate = 6.9%
  27| and Separate schools, Residential, etc = 10.5%.
  28| Indicator 6 – Educational Environments for Students Aged 3-5 with IEPs.  The 2013-2014 state rates showed the
  29| percent of students receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full Inclusion early
  30| childhood program was 39.0%, in a Partial Inclusion Program was “not identified”, a Substantially Separate
  31| Placement (special education class, separate school, or residential facility) was 13.8%, and students not attending
  32| an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was “not identified”.  In
  33| Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate was 34.3%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 48.6%, Substantially
  34| Separate rate was 2.9%, and Related Services was 14.3%.
  35| Indicator 9 & 10  - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special
  36| Education Categories.  In Lunenburg, the stat e determined that there was no disproportionality found in either
  37| case.
  38| Indicator 11 – Initial Evaluation Timelines.   T his indicator measures the % of children with parental consent to
  39| evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is reported for all initial evaluations
  40| initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting year.  Lunenburg was rated 100% in compliance
  41| and the state average for this Indicator was 98.8%.
  42| Indicator 12 – Early Childhood Transition.  T his indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C, found
  43| eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third birthdays.
  44| The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as “Suppresse d”.  Data is suppressed if the
  45| number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is fewer than 10.  In
  46| Lunenburg, only 2 students were referred during 2012 -2013 (we have not been reviewed since 2012 -2013 for
  47| updated data in this area).

===PAGE 105=== (plain, 48 lines)
   1| 105
   2| Indicator 13 – Secondary Transition.  This indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with
   3| an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based upon an age
   4| appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will reasonably enable the
   5| student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's transition services needs.
   6| Lunenburg was rated 100% in compliance  and the state average for this Indicator was 99.2% (we have not been
   7| reviewed since 2011-2012 for updated data in this area).
   8| Indicator 14 – Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs.  This indicator measures the % of students with
   9| IEPs who exited hig h school during the 2012 -13 school year and self -reported post -school engagement in
  10| education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through three measurements:
  11|  Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  12|  Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high
  13| school.
  14|  Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training
  15| program; or competitivel y employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high
  16| school.
  17| Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post -
  18| secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2013 -2014 school year, state targets for the three
  19| measurements of Post -School Outcomes are Measurement A: 45%; Measurement B: 80%; and Measurement C:
  20| 87%.  Lunenburg rates are as follows:  Measurement A = 71.4%, Measurement B = 100%, and Measurement C =
  21| 100%.
  22| The Parent Advisory Council Supporting All Learners (PACSAL) continues to be an active support group

  25| typically held the first Friday of each month, 11:00AM, at the Thomas C. Passios Building, room 13.  The

  27| PACSAL for both parents and teachers in Lunenburg this year. For more information, go to:
  28| https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/special-services/pacsal.

  30| meet the varying needs of our students in the least restrictive environment.  We have an experienced, hard
  31| working staff that works together with parents to provide these varied services.   Our parents are equal partners in
  32| our efforts to develop challenging and focused educational programs for children.  We remain committed to the
  33| Lunenburg Public Schools mission, “We prepa re our students for lifelong learning and responsible community

  41| LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
  42| 2015-2016
  43| The Lunenburg Primary School is devoted to providing quality early childhood educat ion for students
  44| Preschool to 3 rd grade.  Our students range from three years old to nine years old. Our community is made up of
  45| students, parents, teachers and support staff, working together to create an educationally rich and safe learning
  46| environment for our children.
  47| The October 1, 2015 student count reported to the department of Elementary and Secondary Education was five
  48| hundred and three students.  Our Preschool program services thirty-eight three and four year old

===PAGE 106=== (plain, 72 lines)
   1| 106
   2| students. We have five Kindergart en classes servicing ninety -nine students. Class sizes in our
   3| Kindergartens are 20 students.  First grade has one hundred and fifteen students with class sizes ranging from 22 -
   4| 23. Second grade has one hundred and thirty -three students with class sizes ran ging from 26-27 students and the
   5| third grade has one hundred and twenty -two students with class sizes ranging from 24 -25.  We are utilizing all
   6| available space in the building to provide a child-centered educational program for all our students.
   7| We const antly strive to find better ways to meet the collective as well as the individual needs of our
   8| students.  Our Professional Development focused on Teacher Evaluation, Reading, Behavioral Intervention
   9| strategies and our new Everyday Math program materials. A ll of our classroom teachers continued to use
  10| Responsive Classroom strategies as part of our Academic/Social/Emotional Learning Curriculum which includes
  11| morning meetings, guided discovery, rules and logical consequences and academic choice.  Teachers this  past
  12| year completed curriculum maps and served on committees to revise and improve our ELA Curriculum and our
  13| Social Studies Curriculum.
  14| The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to insure
  15| that all stud ents have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are completed in the fall,
  16| winter, and spring for all children in grades K -3 in the areas of reading, writing, and math.  These common
  17| assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of t eacher instruction and drive individual student instruction.
  18| Struggling students are identified quickly and additional resources and instruction are provided to address the
  19| areas of concern identified.  Tiered instructional services are provided in the ar ea of Language Arts and Math.
  20| The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only meet the needs of the students who may be
  21| struggling with learning but those students who are doing well and need to achieve higher levels of learning.
  22| Learning is becoming more personalized and students are being challenged to be confident, independent learners
  23| and problem solvers.
  24| Our third graders participated in the Spring 2015 MCAS Assessment in the areas of ELA and Math.  We
  25| work to make sure that students feel confident and prepared to take this assessment for the first time.  All teachers
  26| have worked over the last several years to prepare them to be independent confident learners.

  26| TURKEY HILL MIDDLE SCHOOL
  27| 2015-2016
  28| Turkey Hill Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong progra ms that support
  29| students’ social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self -motivated, creative,
  30| lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest communication.
  31| The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philosophy of educating the
  32| whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by which all students become
  33| positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in high school. Turkey Hill Middle School is
  34| in its fourth and final year of servicing students in grades four through seven. We look forward to the
  35| opportunities a new middle high school will have for students in the community as we move  to a co-located state
  36| of the art school building in August of 2016.

===PAGE 109=== (plain, 42 lines)
   1| 109
   2| PERCENT OF STUDENTS SCORING IN THE
   3| ADVANCED/PROFICIENT RANGE
   4| Grade Subject THMS State
   5| 4 ELA 57% 53%
   6| 4 Math 46% 47%
   7| 5 ELA 73% 71%
   8| 5 Math 71% 67%
   9| 5 Science/Tech 63% 51%
  10| 6 ELA 74% 71%
  11| 6 Math 67% 62%
  12| 7 ELA 70% 51%
  13| 7 Math 70% 50%
  14| In reviewing the results of the 2015 Spring M.C.A.S. tests, in all tested content areas at all grade levels, we
  15| consistently produced scores that are at or above the state average in percentage of students  who fall in the
  16| advanced and proficient range, in some cases, 19% over the state average. Although we are proud of our students’
  17| performance, we continue to analyze data in our monthly results faculty meetings designed to improve our
  18| instruction for all students. Our faculty has identified areas of improvement based upon the data and implemented
  19| strategies to improve their instructional practices that will help students succeed.
  20| For the third straight year, Turkey Hill Middle School fourth grade teachers  continue to utilize and acknowledge
  21| the benefits of an interdisciplinary teaming model.  This model permits teachers to work together in teams of three
  22| in order to provide focused instruction in the areas of reading and mathematics. Our specially designed  teaming
  23| model allows students to remain with their homeroom teacher for the majority of the day, but designates a
  24| reading, writing, and mathematics teacher on each team. Teachers can develop expertise as content area
  25| specialists and the administration tea m provides specific professional development in each focus area for
  26| continuous improvement and growth. This form of teaming also allows us to create more common experiences at
  27| the grade level through unit planning and shared assessments.
  28| Additionally, teaming helps prepare students for an increased level of expectations in reading, math, and writing
  29| when entering the fourth grade, while continuing to be sensitive to the social and emotional needs of this
  30| population.
  31| We continue to utilize intervention and p ower blocks during the school day to meet the needs of each individual
  32| student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built into our master schedule allow teachers time to
  33| work with students at their own individual skill level.  The philosophy is to challenge students who are high
  34| academic achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have identified weaknesses. Teachers are able to
  35| work with students in a small group to answer individual questions or provide more individualized suppor t.  We
  36| design this support model to allow students to feel more confident, thus, perform better in the classroom.  We will
  37| continue to refine our intervention block times to best meet the needs of our students.
  38| Turkey Hill Middle School continues to offer  enrichment blocks for all students. Enrichment classes provide
  39| students a block of time in which they receive instruction in the areas of developmental health and guidance. Our
  40| Second step developmental guidance program is now consistent in grades four th rough seven and units focus on
  41| healthy decision making, empathy and communication as well bullying prevention to meet the intricate social
  42| needs of our middle school students.

===PAGE 110=== (plain, 51 lines)
   1| 110
   2| Students also receive instruction in physical education, art, library media, a nd music classes that allows them to
   3| explore their strengths and talents. This year, fourth and fifth grade students have been participating in a word
   4| processing enrichment class to improve their keyboarding skills. Students also have the opportunity to si gn up for
   5| and participate in band and chorus during this block. This enrichment block is in addition to the traditional special
   6| area classes students receive, and this block offers students additional physical movement throughout the day,
   7| additional opportunities for hands on activities and more exposure to the arts, theatre and technology throughout
   8| the school day.
   9| We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs developed to
  10| promote social and emotional growt h, self -awareness, and community service. We will continue to utilize
  11| responsive classroom and Developmental Design programs to deliver our social emotional curriculum. Students
  12| meet with teachers in small groups to teach social responsibility and problem solving, as well as developing
  13| empathy and promoting positive self -esteem. Our seventh grade students are participating in themes such as
  14| embracing and respecting diversity and grades four and five students were experiencing themes developed
  15| through our se cond step guidance curriculum. We also incorporated an academic choice time for students in
  16| grades four and five. This is a time developed for students to explore different interests or experiences or utilize
  17| time to complete assignments that required additional time.
  18| We also utilize best practices such as peer mediation and the Big Sibs program to foster and develop positive peer
  19| leadership skills and effective peer mentoring.  All of the social emotional programs have been developed to
  20| allow students to showcase their talents and abilities, grow and improve as individuals, and promote community
  21| outreach for the younger generation.
  22| Turkey Hill Middle school continues to provide educational services and support that ensures student success and
  23| promotes lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to our success is our
  24| talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our
  25| teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning.
  26| Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  27| Respectfully Submitted,
  28| Timothy Santry, Principal

===PAGE 112=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 112
   2| LUNENBURG HIGH SCHOOL
   3| 2015-2016
   4| Lunenburg High School has been a hub for academic and social activity in 2015 -2016. We have begun
   5| transitioning from our current building to the new Lunenburg Middle High School.  The transition process is far
   6| bigger than just moving from one building to another.  With the middle and high school in the same building,
   7| there are some challenges, but many opportunities to enhance the educational experiences of our students.   A lot
   8| of planning and decisions have been made t o ensure that we will be moving into a state -of-the-art school which
   9| will support 21 st century learning.  The building process has gone very smoothly and our students and educators
  10| are already looking forward to the start of school in August.
  11|  As time h as gone on, there is much more of a comfort level and stability in our grade 8 -12 community.  This
  12| year, we oriented 116 eighth grade students, four teachers, and six staff members to our building and school
  13| culture.  With the assistance of 40 student lead ers, we successfully transitioned the eighth grade students into the
  14| LHS culture by having an eighth grade orientation on the first day of school.  We opened the year, as we do every
  15| year, with the District Attorney and Assistant District Attorney from Wor cester County to discuss responsible
  16| Internet use, Facebook, cyber-bullying, Internet safety, drug use, and the power of making good choices with our
  17| incoming students and parents.
  18| NEASC ACCREDITATION
  19| Lunenburg High School completed a Five Year Report for the Commission on Public Secondary Schools of the
  20| New England Association of Schools and Colleges.  In 2010, the Commission recommended that the school
  21| receive continued accreditation in the Association. The Commission's decision was based upon review of an
  22| evaluation report prepared by a visiting committee in April 2010.  The Two Year Progress Report, submitted in
  23| January, 2013 received high marks from the Commission reviewing our submission.  The Mid -Cycle Progress
  24| Report was reviewed and accepted by the Commission.  One more Special Progress Report needs to be filed with
  25| the Commission in September.  It will certify that the building project is complete and LHS will be taken off of
  26| “Warning” status and given full accreditation.
  27| SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
  28| Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of fundraising and collection
  29| endeavors. In the fall, three staff members were involved in a fundraiser to raise money for the Lunenburg Food
  30| Pantry.  Money was collected during lunch and the staff member who raised the most money, got a pie in the face
  31| from a student at the Thanksgiving Academic Achievement Rally.  Fun was had by all, and all of the proceeds
  32| were donated for the holiday season.
  33| Lunenburg High School received a generous donation in the amount of $500 from Mr. Mike’s Mobil in
  34| Lunenburg, MA.  The grant is to be used toward STEM initiatives at the high school.  We are in the process of
  35| purchasing a new robot for our Robotics class.
  36| Hannaford Helps Schools made a donation of $1,000 last spring, which was earmarked for student activities.  In
  37| addition, Hannaford donated an additional $500 from their receipt sales, which will benefit other activities at
  38| LHS.
  39| In March, the National Honor Society will be having its annual Blood Drive.  Students sixteen and over (and staff
  40| members) will be able to participate in the Blood Drive with parent permission.
  41| The Media 2.0 Club has launched the Knightly News, which is an online newspaper.  Students report on school
  42| activities, post film reviews, write political commentaries, and upload pictures of things going on inside and
  43| outside of LHS.
  44| LHS has held evening presentations for parents/guardians on the following topics:
  45|  Planning for High School (grade 8)

===PAGE 113=== (plain, 44 lines)
   1| 113
   2|  Early College Planning (grades 9-10)
   3|  Post-Secondary Education Planning (grade 11)
   4|  College Application Process (grade 12)
   5|  Financing your Education/Financial Aid (grades 8-12)
   6| LHS hosted 40 college admissions counselors in the school counseling office to visit wi th LHS students.  In
   7| addition, we held two “Decision Day” programs with Fitchburg State University and Mount Wachusett
   8| Community College.
   9| MCAS RESULTS
  10| We are pleased to announce that Lunenburg High School is now recognized by the Department of Elementary and
  11| Secondary Education as a Level 1 school.  As a Level 1 school, we are among the highest performing schools in
  12| the Commonwealth and shows that we are meeting our gap narrowing goals.  This is a tribute to all of the hard
  13| work of our students, teachers, and the strong adult influences in our students’ lives.
  14| Lunenburg High School – 2015 MCAS Results (Grade 8)
  15| Subject Test Advanced/Proficient State Average (Adv/Pro)
  16| ELA 88% 80%
  17| Math 71% 60%
  18| Science/Technology 58% 42%
  19| Lunenburg High School - 2015 MCAS Results (Grade 10)
  20| Subject Test Advanced/Proficient State Average (Adv/Pro)
  21| ELA 98% 91%
  22| Math 89% 78%
  23| Science/Technology 86% 71%
  24| When you combine the percentage bands of the advanced and proficient range, eighth grade students scored 88%
  25| on t he ELA test.  In Mathematics, 71% of eighth grade students scored advanced/proficient.  In
  26| Science/Technology, 58% of eighth grade students scored advanced/proficient.  Curriculum and instruction were
  27| a focus for us last year in the area of Science in the eighth grade and scores rose by 20%.
  28| When you combine the percentage bands of the advanced and proficient range, LHS tenth grade students scored
  29| 98% on the ELA test.  In Mathematics, tenth grade students scored 89% advanced/proficient. In
  30| Science/Technology/Engineering, tenth grade students scored 86% advanced/proficient.  We are extremely proud
  31| of our students and teachers for their continued improvement, and we will work to move all students to the level
  32| of proficiency as measured by MCAS and our goal of  college and career ready as they move through our
  33| curriculum.
  34| We were also very pleased to learn that 32 seniors at Lunenburg High School received the state’s John and
  35| Abigail Adams Scholarship this year.  The scholarship is available to students whose  MCAS performance puts
  36| them at the top 25 percent of their district.  To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and
  37| Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the English and Math exams.
  38| HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES
  39| Homecoming was once again a great town-wide celebration, culminating with a beautiful parade complete with
  40| floats representing all four high school classes.  While the eighth grade did not have a float in the parade, they did
  41| participate in the Homecoming celebration.  Many of our sports teams qualified for tournament and district play.
  42| The LHS Mock trial team has advanced to the regionals this year after going 3-0 in local trials.  The Latin club,
  43| marching band, jazz band and the math team represented LHS in numerous competitions; the 50th annual
  44| Competitive Class Plays delighted the audience, and the school-wide play, A Date With Judy, was well received.

===PAGE 114=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 114
   2| The Mr. LHS talent show, a hypnotist (sponsored by the Class of 2018), a Lock -In (sponsored by the Class of
   3| 2017), and the Pops and Senior Showcase are scheduled for the spring.
   4| COMMUNITY BUILDING
   5| National Honor Society successfully supported the “Giftmart” drive during the holidays, helping to provide joy to
   6| needy children and families in conjunction with local agencies.  The World Hunger Task Force will be raising
   7| money through Penny Wars this spring.  The money will be donated toward the fight against hunger.  Also, this
   8| winter, one of our eighth grade students participated in Project 351.  She spent the day in Boston with hundreds of
   9| eighth grade students, giving back to the community.
  10| In October, we brought Survive the Drive, to LHS.   Survive the Drive highlighted the dangers that our students
  11| (and society face) with distracted driving.  There was a big emphasis on the dangers of texting while driving.  The
  12| presentation was made possible by a donation from North End Subaru.  A crashed car was located in the back
  13| parking lot of the school.  The car was placed at the school by Qualit y Towing and Recovery.  SADD provided
  14| the students with information about distracted driving during the course of the week and had students and staff
  15| pledge to not text and drive.  During an Advisory period, SADD showed the short video, The Last Text.
  16| We have started an Advisory program at LHS.  One goal of Advisory is to have students have at least one adult
  17| who will get to know him/her over the course of four years.  Students were randomly distributed into small grade-
  18| level groups  who they  will stay wi th during the four year period.  Another goal of Advisory is to develop
  19| relationships with students of different backgrounds and social groups so that students can get to know each other
  20| and develop an appreciation of the diversity that we do have in Lunenburg.
  21| CURRICULUM
  22| The staff at Lunenburg High School focused their professional development activities in 2015 -2016 working on
  23| curriculum development, curriculum mapping, District -Determined Measures, vertical teaming, and preparing to
  24| move into the new building.

===PAGE 117=== (plain, 48 lines)
   1| 117
   2| The 2014 -2015 school year was my first year as Superintendent -Director of Montachusett Regional
   3| Vocational Technical School District, and it seemed to come and go in a minute ; I would be remiss if I did
   4| not take time to reflect upon the numerous achievements our talented students, faculty and staff
   5| accomplished throughout the school year.  These awards, recognitions, and distinctions are the result of years
   6| of high quality education and vocational -technical training.  They are the hallmark of a Monty Tech
   7| education, earned by both individuals’ and team efforts.  While they are, in deed, an honor, they are simply a
   8| snapshot of the wonderful experiences happening on a day -to-day basis here at Monty Tech.  I am delighted
   9| to present the D istrict’s 2014 -2015 annual report to you, highligh ting some of the year’s most notable
  10| achievements, including:
  11|  A talented team of three Monty Tech Engineering students won the gold medal for Engineering
  12| Technology and Design at the SkillsUSA national competitions held in Louisville, KY.  Nic olas Wicker
  13| of Fitchburg, Leon Gaulin of Winchendon and Devan Kumar, also of Winchendon, led by instructor
  14| Dennis Cormier, created a portable game console designed with retro gaming in mind.  The team has
  15| developed patents on their prize-winning efforts, and continues to expand their business and engineering
  16| acumen.
  17|  The Machine Technology program aligned curriculum t o MASSMEP (Massachusetts Manufacturing
  18| Extension Partnership) standards, and as a result students are now able to earn Level 1 and Level 2
  19| Applied Manufac turing Technology Pathway Certifications.  Having these certifications will ensure
  20| program graduates stand -out when they enter the workforce upon graduation, as employers are better
  21| able to verify technical skills and expertise with this widely accepted tr aining model.  Also, our
  22| instructors are now able to access the  manufacturing curriculum developed through W orcester
  23| Polytechnic Institute, adding rigor and relevance to our very popular Machine Technology program.
  24|  The Monty Tech Auto Body/ Collision Repai r Department was featured in the April 2015 edition of the
  25| New England Automotive Report, applauded for  its dedication to excellence.  Molly Brodeur, President
  26| of the Alliance of Automotive Service Providers, Massachusetts cited the program’s innovative
  27| technologies, spacious facilities, and specialized equipment as among the best in New England.
  28|  Outpacing more than 1,000 teams across the United State s, and competing in three categories:  network
  29| security, digital forensics, and Cisco networking, a team of six talented students captured first place at
  30| the CyberPatriot event, a national high school cyber defense competition, founded by the Air Force
  31| Association.
  32|  Students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to demonstrate high
  33| academic achievement.  In the spring of 2015, Monty Tech’s passing rate on the MCAS English
  34| Language Arts exam was 100%, Mathematics 98%, and Biology 98%.
  35| We are so proud of the educational programs offered here at Monty Tech, yet we continually strive to
  36| improve upon them.  Collaborating with area colleges and universities, we are ensuring o ur curriculum and
  37| instruction are rigorous and relevant.  Sharing best practices with vocational-technical colleagues from across
  38| the state – and nation – we give and take some of the best ideas, with one thing in mind – what is best for our
  39| students.  And what is best, I believe, is preparing students for both college and career.
  40| We hope you will find this report a comprehensive review of the quality education you have come to know
  41| and expect from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School.  You may notice that each of the
  42| eighteen member cities and towns are reflected in this report, and that students performed services in almost
  43| every community last year.  Providing our students with an opportunity to give back to the communities that
  44| support them – and support our school – is a pleasure.
  45|         Respectfully submitted,
  46|         __________________________________
  47|         Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D.
  48| Superintendent-Director

   2| Our Mission
   3| Every student will graduate from M ontachusett Regional Vocational  Technical School with the skills,
   4| knowledge, and abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
   5| Our District
   6| Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is a four -year career and technical high school serving
   7| the member towns of:
   8| Ashburnham
   9| Ashby
  10| Athol
  11| Barre
  12| Fitchburg
  13| Gardner
  14| Harvard
  15| Holden
  16| Hubbardston
  17| Lunenburg
  18| Petersham
  19| Phillipston
  20| Princeton
  21| Royalston
  22| Sterling
  23| Templeton
  24| Westminster
  25| Winchendon
  26| Leadership
  27| The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented
  28| administrators whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise
  29| contribute to the success of the school.  Working co llaboratively, and under the direction of the
  30| Superintendent and Principal, the team has been able to transform the school into one of the most sought -
  31| after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.
  32| Sheila M. Harrity, Superintendent-Director
  33| Tom Browne, Principal
  34| Dayana Carlson, Assistant Principal
  35| Tammy Crockett, Business Manager
  36| Francine Duncan, Director of Technology
  37| Christina Favreau, Director of Academic Programs
  38| Jim Hachey, Director of Vocational Programs
  39| Richard Ikonen, Director of Facilities
  40| Katy Whitaker, Development Coordinator
  41| Victoria Zarozinski, Director of Student Support Services
  42| Enrollment
  43| On June 1, 2015, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,418 students in grades nine through twelve.
  44| Students are represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (61), Ashby (35), Athol (88),
  45| Barre (37), Fitchburg (368), Gardner (146), Har vard (5), Holden (71), Hubbardston (62), Lunenburg (78),
  46| Petersham (4), Phillipston (18), Princeton (20), Royalston (18), Sterling (61), Templeton (93), Westminster
  47| (81), and Winchendon (146). The remaining 26 students were from out -of-district towns, incl uding Ayer,
  48| Clinton, Lancaster, Leominster, Orange, Shirley, Webster and Worcester.
  49| Throughout 2014-2015, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community
  50| members to learn about and visit the school.  In October 2014, app roximately 1,000 district eighth graders
  51| participated in the annual “Tour Day” event.   Students toured our twenty vocational-technical areas and
  52| learned about the school’s challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular
  53| programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return in the evening with
  54| their family members, to further explore the facilities and talk with staff members.
  55| Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications for
  56| admissions.  2014-2015 proved to be an exceptionally busy year for her, as the school received 631
  57| applications for admission.  Of those, 575 were from students hoping to enter our incoming freshman class.

===PAGE 119=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 119
   2| The balance of the applications came from students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen.   Because
   3| there are only a limited number of students the school can accept each year, 338 freshmen and 3
   4| upperclassmen were admitted.
   5| The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P. ) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit
   6| Monty Tech after school, and participate in hands -on learning experiences across a variety of
   7| vocational/technical areas. The program continued to attract a large number of students during the 2014-2015
   8| school year, serving approximately 600 area students.
   9| Class of 2015 Awards
  10| Members of the Class of 2015 were awarded approximately $55,000 in scholarships.   The Monty Tech
  11| Foundation generously provided $35,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $200
  12| to $2,000.  The Foundation also awarded $6,000 to the Practical Nursing graduates.  Once again, local and
  13| state organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty
  14| Tech graduates in the form of financial donations. The School Committee, administration, facu lty, and
  15| graduates themselves, are grateful for this support.
  16| Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher
  17| education.  Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits t hrough a number of
  18| articulation agreements with public and private colleges across the country.   Approximately 54% of the
  19| graduating class of 2015 reported plans to enroll at either a 2 -year college, 4 -year college/university, or a
  20| technical/trade school upon graduation.  By earning college credits while still in high school, these students
  21| will benefit by saving both time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  22| Financial Report
  23| In an effort to develop a cost -effective budget for the fi scal year 2014 -2015, a great deal of effort was put
  24| forth by the School Committee, administration, and staff.   The final fiscal year 2014 -2015 Educational Plan
  25| totaled $25,640,833, which represents a 3.5% increase over the 2013 -2014 Educational Plan.  The District’s
  26| FY15 budget, once again, only meets the minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law
  27| Chapter 70.
  28| The District was audited in August 2015 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of
  29| Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and a very good report is anticipated.
  30| Grants and Contracts
  31| Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis.   These funds assist in providing many
  32| educational and social services to the student population.   For fiscal year 2015, state and federal grant
  33| sources provided the school with $866,668.   Programs funded by these grants include:   Essential Health
  34| Services, Social Intervention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and
  35| Program Improvement,  Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, and Perkins Postsecondary
  36| Education.  Using these allocation funds, the school was able to purchase a variety of instructional
  37| technology, equipment and supplies to enhance the learning experience across bo th academic and vocational
  38| programs.
  39| Academic Achievement
  40| In 2014 -2015, students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to demonstrate
  41| high academic achievement, earning commendable MCAS scores in English Language Arts, Mathematics
  42| and Biology.  For Spring 2015, Monty Tech’s passing rate o n the English Language Arts was 100%,
  43| Mathematics 98%, Biology 98%.
  44| The district is particularly proud of the number of students earning Advanced scores, and is pleased to report
  45| significant increases in this category.  On the English Language Arts  exam, students scoring Advanced

  23| The district continues to make progress toward narrowing proficiency gaps.  Students in all subgroups have
  24| met their progress and performance targets, securing the school’s Level 1 Status distinction.
  25| Vocational Projects in the District Communities
  26| Unlike students in traditional, comprehensive high schools, students at Monty Tech are asked to put their
  27| education into practice on a daily basis.  Students across the twenty different vocational -technical education
  28| programs are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles, making
  29| walkways more accessible, and performing countless community services.
  30| The 2014-2015 school year was a busy one for our vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide
  31| practical, hands -on learning experiences for our students, while helping to impr ove each of the eighteen
  32| member communities.
  33| Auto Body:  C ollision Repair Technology :  A highlight of the 2014 -2015 school year was the addition of
  34| new instructor, Michael Forhan, who brings wonderful industry experience and expertise to the popular Auto
  35| Body program.  The program received an I -CAR Education Foundation Grant, and as a result, sophomores
  36| and juniors will receive a Level I I -CAR certification.  Students detailed, repaired, and repainted more than
  37| eighty automobiles and trucks, including a dump truck for the  Winchendon Housing Authority.  Six ski
  38| chairs were coated for the Johnny Appleseed Trail Association , a nd trail brackets were painted for
  39| Baldwinville Department of Conservation. A state -of-the-art fresh -air system was installed in the spray
  40| booth, providing our students with clean air while working in the booth, eliminating the need for respirators.
  41| (Total enrollment: 62; 34 males, 28 females)

===PAGE 121=== (plain, 49 lines)
   1| 121
   2| Automotive Technology : As in past years, the Automotive Technology program continues to serv ice a
   3| variety of vehicles for faculty, staff, municipalities, and district residents.  2014-2015 proved to be a very

  31| Student Support Services
  32| During the 201 4-2015 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided
  33| special services to approximately three hundred and fifteen students – measuring progress of over two
  34| hundred students on Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and just under one  hundred students adhering to
  35| individualized Section 504 plans.   While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses
  36| special education, the department provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
  37| The department includes a ful l-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state -mandated
  38| health screening exams, and provides, when necessary, health information to the special education team for a
  39| student’s IEP meeting.   The department benefits from a full -time school social worker who participates in
  40| departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family issues,
  41| homelessness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing.  The school is also fortunate to have on
  42| staff a full-time psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial evaluation or who
  43| require a three -year re -evaluation. In addition, we have a full -time speech language pathologist, who is
  44| available to assist students with disabilities, asses s these students and consult with teachers.   Our students
  45| also have access to the services of a full -time adjustment counselor and part-time school psychologist. All of
  46| these individuals are available for scheduled counseling sessions and mental health eme rgency treatment, as
  47| well as crisis intervention.

   7| Technology
   8| In 2014-2015 Monty Tech continued the process of improving our network infrastructure to meet future
   9| needs.  Additional access points were added to our wireless network to broaden connectivity.  Internet
  10| bandwidth was increased, and multiple carriers were co ntracted to reduce the possibility of Internet
  11| downtime.  A back up generator was installed and connected to our network servers to decrease the
  12| possibility of unexpected shutdown of vital equipment.
  13| Monty Tech successfully completed two Massachusetts Department of Elementary and Secondary Education
  14| initiatives involving technology.  Pilot testing for PARCC, an online student assessment program, was
  15| conducted in Spring 2015, and state reporting of SIMS (student data) and SCS (student course schedule data)
  16| via SIF  (Schools Interoperability Framework) commenced in March 2015.
  17| Monty Tech continued the process of enhancing and automating procedures.  Teachers began using x2
  18| Aspen’s Teacher Recommendations to enter student course requests, and the school imp roved parent
  19| communication by including email addresses as part of our parent/student notification system.  The school
  20| also initiated improvements to technical support through the automation of computer builds and updates.
  21| Monty Tech began the transition of becoming a Google for Education school by changing our email system
  22| to Gmail for staff and students.   Three Chromebook mobile labs were purchased late in the school year and
  23| will be ready for classroom use in 2015 -2016.  Monty Tech will plan profession al development and support
  24| for additional Google apps as they are introduced during 2015 -2016, and will consider a Chromebook 1:1
