  18|  Twelve stu dents and five instructors attended the National Leadership & Skills Conference in
  19| Louisville, KY. from June 22 – 27, 2015.
  20|  A team of three talented Engineering Technology students received a Gold Medal for their
  21| performance at the National Engineering T echnology/Design Contest.  Working closely with their
  22| instructor, Dennis Cormier, Nicolas Wicker  (Class of 2015), Leon Gaulin  (Class of 2017), and
  23| Devan Kumar (Class of 2017) brought home this well-deserved honor.
  24|  Taryne Swan and Cassandra Campbell were also recognized at the National Conference, receiving
  25| the President’s Volunteer Service Award, Gold Standard
  26|  Throughout the year, students participated in a number of community service projects, including: a
  27| “Change for Children” Campaign; a holiday party and celebration for children in the Fitchburg area;
  28| Salvation Army “Christmas Angels” were distributed to collect toys and gifts for underprivileged
  29| children; Raffle to benefit Be Like Brit Foundation and The Doug Flutie Jr. Foundation for Autism.
  30| Marine Corps JROTC
  31| The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROTC) had a sensational school
  32| year.  All program objectives for the 2014 -2015 school year were achieved, most notable was the JROTC
  33| Cyber Security Team, led by First  Sergeant Paul Jornet and Information Technology Instructor Richard
  34| Duncan, once again capturing national recognition by placing first in the Air Force Association’s National
  35| Cyber Security Competition held in Washington, DC.
  36| The 2014-2015 Corps of Cadets  completed over eighty-eight hundred hours (8800+) of community service
  37| throughout the district. The corps conducted a major canned food drive, worked side-by-side with the Marine
  38| Corps Reserve in a national Toys 4 Tots campaign, and spent four weekends wo rking with the local
  39| Salvation Army helping to raise over $26,000 for needy families.  The cadets conducted their annual 10 mile
  40| “March-A-Thon” to support NEADS (National Education of Assistance Dogs Services) from Princeton, MA,
  41| raising $25,000 for their Service Dogs for Veterans program.
  42| Sixty Monty Tech cadets attended a leadership camp at the Citadel in Charleston, SC.  The camp provided

===PAGE 129=== (plain, 39 lines)
   1| 129
   2| cadets with individual and team development opportunities, as well as the opportunity to visit many
   3| historical sites.
   4| During the summer of 2015, our Cadet Cyber Team was again asked to support a Cyber STEM camp for the
   5| National Marine Corps JROTC program. The Cyber Team coordinated the 12 -day camp that focused on
   6| defending cyber networks from attacks. The camp also of fered an introduction to robotics programming
   7| using the VEX Robotics System.  The camp was attended by 200 students, representing thirty-five states.
   8| The Cyber team along with community financial support and a group of Monty Tech staff volunteers also
   9| conducted a week-long Cyber Security camp for 50 middle school students. The camp, which was offered at
  10| no cost to the students, focused on computer operating systems, security and cyber ethics.  In partnership
  11| with Fitchburg State University and Mount Wach usett Community College, the students also had an
  12| opportunity to visit both campuses to explore STEM pathways.
  13| The Monty Tech JROTC program received an incredible honor, having been named a Marine Corps Honor
  14| School by the Commanding General Marine Corps Training & Education Command.  Only ten percent of
  15| Marine Corps JROTC programs nationwide receive this recognition.  The selection was based on several
  16| criteria, including cadet citizenship, the percentage of cadets involved in student government organizat ions,
  17| the number of cadets active in school activities and clubs, the number of community service hours for the
  18| unit as a whole and per cadet, the number of organizations assisted by the unit, and scholastic achievements
  19| including the number of academic awards and scholarships given to cadets.  The units were also measured by
  20| participation in public affairs events, drill team competitions, rifle team competitions, physical fitness team
  21| competitions, and field trips.
  22| Because of this prestigious status, we  are pleased to announce that the Monty Tech JROTC instructors unit
  23| will now have the opportunity to make nomination recommendations to the U.S. Naval Academy, U.S. Air
  24| Force Academy, and the Military Academy at West Point under the Honor School Category.
  25| Women in Technology
  26| Monty Tech is one of the original members of the North Central Massachusetts Women In Technology
  27| Program and the 2014 -2015 school year marked the school’s 13 th year in this innovative school -to-business
  28| partnership.  The highly successful collaborative program continues to grow with membership including
  29| students from six area high schools, introducing female students to well -paying careers in business and
  30| technology.  Participants spend two days a month working on real -world work projects under the mentorship
  31| of company managers at SimplexGrinnell and Tyco Safety Products in Westminster, subsidiaries of Tyco
  32| International, a Fortune 500 company.
  33| The program has been so successful in its mission of providing a proven pathway to corporate America, that
  34| it was recently recognized as one of the region’s premier experience -based educational programs of its kind.
  35| Affiliation with the program, and skills acquired through participation, open doors to career opportunities not
  36| otherwise available to high school students. Graduates of the program are also equipped with a foundation to
  37| better meet the challenges of an ever-changing and demanding work force.
  38| Each year, graduates of the program go on to rewarding and well -paying careers, made possible by this
  39| unique experience.

   7| Postgraduate and Continuing Studies
   8| The Post Graduate and Continuing Studies Program, also known as Monty Tech Nites, continues to update and
   9| add courses that emphasize a commitment to excellence by offering affordable, quality, and enjoyable educational
  10| experiences.  For the f all of 2014, M onty Tech offered 78 in -house classes with 626 registrations.  We also
  11| offered a number of online course options, with a total  of 27 registrations.  For the s pring 2015 semester, we
  12| offered 73 in-house courses with 568 registrations and an additional 47 registrations for online programs.
  13| In March 201 5 the postgraduate program successfully graduated our third class of nine Emergency Medical
  14| Technicians.  The very successful EMT program is pleased to report increased acceptance/ enrollment numbers,
  15| as the program accepted a class of 16 students for the 2015-2016 school year.
  16| The Director of the Postgraduate & Continuing Studies Program actively seeks information to develop new (and
  17| expand existing) certificate and licensure programs that align with region al workforce needs and employment
  18| trends.
  19| Practical Nursing Program
  20| The Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely, ethically and in a caring
  21| manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status  in diverse health care settings.  This
  22| mission,which is consistent with the philosophy and goals of the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical
  23| School District, accomplishes the following:
  24| · Identifies a strong relationship between academic and vocational preparation
  25| · Stresses the importance of developing critical thinking skills to function safely, effectively, and
  26| productively in an ever-changing technical and diverse society
  27| · Supports the maintenance of a positive and caring learning and practice environment
  28| On June 18, 2015, the Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program saw one of its largest graduating classes - forty (40)
  29| students - complete the Practical Nursing Program and enter the nursing profession.  The class achieved an initial
  30| NCLEX pass rate of 88%, and just over 75% of the 2015 graduates are currently working i n the North Central
  31| area, in various health care settings including long-term care, sub -acute care, clinics, medical offices, and prison
  32| health care.
  33| The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Prog ram continues to develop the “LPN to BSN Bridge” relationship with
  34| Fitchburg State University.  Both institutions were awarded a grant from the Massachusetts Department of Higher
  35| Education, supporting the "Nurse of the Future" initiative. The grant funding  will assist veterans hoping to
  36| transition from military to civilian healthcare fields, granting academic credits for previous military training and
  37| experience in related military specialties.
  38| In January, the program added a technology component called Shadow Health, a virtual patient-centered clinical
  39| experience software package.  Students were asked to complete assignments at home, and were assessed
  40| according to their patient/student interaction and clinical practice interactions.  The

===PAGE 132=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 132
   2| added technology proved to be beneficial, allowing the educational program to continue despite the
   3| challenging weather and school closings.
   4| Finally, the Monty Tech PN Class of 2015 completed a meaningful community service project , adopting a
   5| Monty Tech family in need re ferred by the school’s Student Support Services  Department. The nursing
   6| students purchased holiday gifts and food for the family, making sure they were consistent with the family
   7| requests and needs.
   8| Looking Ahead
   9| While the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District educational community is certainly
  10| proud of the achievements of our talented st udents, faculty, and staff, we continue to have an eye toward the
  11| future, always committed to improving our vocational and academic programm ing, strengthening key
  12| partnerships, and maintaining facilities that contribute to student success and achievement.  As we look
  13| ahead, there are a number of programs and initiatives that we expect will have a positive impact on our
  14| school and students for years to come.
  15| Expand partnerships with area businesses:  To support the school’s popular Business Technology program,
  16| specifically addressing the financial elements found within the program’s frameworks, s chool leaders began
  17| exploring opportunities to br ing banking to Monty Tech students.  In collaboration with Workers’ Credit
  18| Union, school officials are pleased to announce a developing partnership that will result in a full -service
  19| branch located on the school’s campus, offering students opportunities to develop authentic financial literacy
  20| skills and experience while still in high school.  Additionally, students studying in the school’s Business
  21| Technology program will be trained as bank tellers, effectively preparing them for entry -level jobs in
  22| banking institutions across North Central Massachusetts.
  23| Expand partnerships with area colleges and universities :  Students in Monty Tech’s very busy Health
  24| Occupations program may just be getting busier in the coming years.  While the program already off ered a
  25| number of industry -recognized credentials, school leaders began working with college partner, Mount
  26| Wachusett Community College, to determine if a college -level Emergency Medical Technician (EMT)
  27| training program could be embedded into the senior ye ar of study.  We are pleased to announce  that this
  28| collaboration has resulted in an agreement that will effectively allow seniors to earn 8 college credits, at no
  29| charge, and the opportunity to sit for the certification exam in June 2016.  By providing additional career
  30| pathways in the medical field, and access to college level coursework while still enrolled in high school,
  31| school administrators and teachers are confident that program gra duates will not only be more qualified for
  32| entry level positions, they will be more apt to find an area of interest within the medical field, and continue to
  33| refine the technical skills they developed while at Monty Tech.
  34| Expand Access to Instructional Technology:  A Monty Tech education is effective because efforts are ma de
  35| to continually access our programming, instructional equipment, and our educational resources.  Through
  36| this assessment, it has become clear that while so many students have access to technology away from the
  37| school, many do not. It is important, then, to arm every student with the tools necessary to succeed not only
  38| in the workplace, but in the classroom , as well.  The district is pleased to announce a large -scale technology
  39| initiative that began in August 2015, when every educator at Monty Tech was provided with a Chromebook
  40| and individualized professi onal development on how to best incorporate this added technology into daily
  41| instruction.  So many teachers have embraced the concept, and continued to explore learning opportunities
  42| that have resulted in classroom instruction that better reflects our 21 st Century school.  The Monty Tech
  43| technology initiative continues, and in August 2016, every student at Monty Tech will also be provided with
  44| a Chromeb ook to support his/her learning, more efficiently commu nicate with teachers, and to promote
  45| collaboration among student peers.

===PAGE 133=== (plain, 55 lines)
   1| 133
   2| Expand vocational-technical educational opportunities:  As you know, Montachusett Regional Vocational
   3| Technical School is home to twenty vocational -technical programs, and currently serves over 1,400 students
   4| from eighteen cities and towns in North Central Massachusetts.  We are so proud of our students and
   5| accomplishments, and every year we strive to provide only the best educational programs, both academic and
   6| vocational, to each an d every student.  As we review our student demographic, the regional workforce data,
   7| and feedback we have received from students interested in attending our school, we consider the benefits of
   8| adding new vocational programs.  We are pleased to announce that Monty Tech is in the development stages
   9| of bringing a new program – our 21 st program – to the school  – Animal/Veterinary Science.  The new
  10| program will r espond to much -needed workforce training and community services, and will effectively
  11| prepare students to enter both college and career pathways upon graduation .  Students and instructors, in
  12| collaboration with program partner, Becker College, will effectively learn concepts associated with a high
  13| school veterinary assisting program, while running a fully -functioning veterinary clinic, designed to provide
  14| care for pets  in low -income families.  Students will have access to college level coursework, and earn
  15| industry-recognized credentials, so that they may either advance to related post-secondary programs, or enter
  16| into the workforce  to fill the projected 25.32% growth in veterinary technician  opportunities through 2022.
  17| While this program is only in its development stages, a great deal of work has already been done to assure its
  18| success.  Student surveys h ave been collected, collaborative working groups have been established, and
  19| industry experts across North Central Massachusetts have been called upon to offer expertise to help ensure
  20| the innovative facility is designed with both education and service in m ind, and that all equipment and
  21| services align with industry standards.

===PAGE 144=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 144
   2| ARTICLE 26.  To see if the Town will vote to amend the General Bylaws of the Town by adding Section
   3| XXXII, entitled Architectural Preservation District, as follows:
   4| Section XXXII ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION DISTRICT
   5| Section 1: Purpose
   6| This bylaw is enacted under the Home Rule Amendment of the Massachusetts Constitution for the purposes of:
   7| (a)  Preserving, restoring and protecting groups of historically and/or architecturally significant buildings and the
   8| characteristics of their neighborhoods that are important to the town’s architectural, cultural, economic, political
   9| and/or social history;
  10| (b)  Fostering wider public knowledge and appreciation of such neighborhoods and Contributing Properties;
  11| (c)  Limiting the detrimental effect of alterations (substantial & minor), additions, and demolitions on the character
  12| of such Contributing Properties and their neighborhood settings;
  13| (d) Reviewing proposed alterations (substantial and minor) including demolition of or to any Contributing Pro perty
  14| located within the APD, for appropriateness and compatibility with the existing buildings, setting and neighborhood
  15| character; and
  16| (e) Facilitating the protection of the APD through a regulatory review.
  17| To achieve these purposes, the Town of Lunenburg may designate APDs to be administered as set forth in this
  18| bylaw.
  19| Section 2: Definitions
  20| For the purpose of this bylaw, the terms and words listed below shall have the following meaning:
  21| ADDITION: Any addition to a principal or accessory Contributing Property that is enclosed by walls and which
  22| increases the existing gross floor area.
  23| ADMINISTRATIVE REVIEW/DETERMINATION OF APPLICABILITY: Pursuant to Section 7 of this
  24| bylaw, a process to review proposed alterations to Contributing Properties and iden tify the need for Binding Review.
  25| AGGRIEVED PARTY: The owner of the subject property or an owner of real property located within the APD or
  26| within three hundred feet of the subject property.
  27| ALTERATION, MINOR: Replacement, modification or material change to existing architectural features on a
  28| Contributing Property, which retains the character of the property as determined by the Architectural Preservation
  29| District Commission. Examples include, but are not limited to windows, doors, roof material and trim work.
  30| ALTERATION, SUBSTANTIAL: A change to a Contributing Property or part thereof, such as removal,
  31| reconstruction, restoration, replication, rehabilitation, addition, or demolition; and/or a change to a site that includes,
  32| enlarging or moving a building and other similar activities.
  33| ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION DISTRICT (“APD”): An area of historic and /or architectural
  34| significance determined by the Town’s residents to be worthy of preservation.
  35| BINDING REVIEW: Pursuant to Section 8 of this bylaw, a mandatory process which applies to substantial
  36| alterations of contributing properties, including demolition, in the APD.
  37| BUILDING: A combination of materials having a roof, the purpose of which is the shelter of persons, animals,
  38| property, or processes, including any other permanent structure.
  39| CERTIFICATE TO ALTER: A document issued by the APDC allowing Alterations pursuant to Section 8 of this
  40| bylaw.
  41| CONTRIBUTING PROPERTY: Any building or stone wall within an APD, which is older than 75 years and
  42| contributes to the Architectural Preservation District’s historical character as determined by the Architectural
  43| Preservation District Commission.

===PAGE 145=== (plain, 22 lines)
   1| 145
   2| DEMOLITION: The act of removing or razing structures or significant parts of structures, or commencing the work of total
   3| or substantial destruction with the intent of completing the same.
   4| DEMOLITION BY NEGLECT: Destruction by failure to maintain roof, siding, foundation or any other aspect that requires
   5| upkeep, which could ultimately result in the Contributing Property being condemned. These include but are not limited to:
   6| 1. Facades which may fall and injure members of the public or property.
   7| 2. Deteriorated or inadequate foundation, deteriorated flooring or floor supports, deteriorated  walls or other vertical
   8| structural supports.
   9| 3. Members of ceilings, roofs, ceiling and roof supports or other horizontal members which sag, split or buckle due to
  10| deterioration.
  11| 4. Deteriorated or ineffective waterproofing of exterior walls, roof, foundations or floors, including broken windows or
  12| doors.
  13| 5. Improper or insufficient waterproofing or exterior wall covering, including lack of paint or weathering due to lack of
  14| paint or other protective covering.
  15| 6. Any fault or defect in the Contributing Property caused by alteration or neglect of maintenance, which renders it not
  16| properly watertight or structurally unsafe.
  17| 7. Fireplaces or chimneys which list, bulge or settle due to deterioration.
  18| DESIGN GUIDELINES: A public document which provides further detail and illustration of the design standards
  19| established in Section 9 of this bylaw. Such guidelines, by facilitating the APDC’s determination as to whether proposed
  20| alterations are appropriate and compatible with the existing Contributing Property’s, setting and neigh borhood character of
  21| the APD, will ensure fair, reasonable and objective preservation practices by the APDC.
  22| DRAFT MAP

===PAGE 146=== (plain, 55 lines)
   1| 146
   2| Section 3: Designation of Architectural Preservation District (APD)
   3| The APD shall, by Town Meeting vote, encompass the areas shown on the map entitled “Town of Lunenburg, Architectural
   4| Preservation District,” and appended to this bylaw. Amendments to the district boundaries may be made only by vote of
   5| Town Meeting.
   6| Section 4: Architectural Preservation District Commission (APDC)
   7| The Board of Selectmen shall appoint a five-member APDC to oversee the APD pursuant to this bylaw. Initially, two
   8| members shall be appointed for one year, two for two years, and one for three years; each successive appointmen t shall be
   9| made for three years. The Board of Selectmen may also appoint up to two alternate members, initially for terms of one, two
  10| and three years, and for three-year terms thereafter. If members are absent, unable to act, or recused from voting due to a
  11| conflict of interest, the APDC chair may designate one or both of the alternate members to serve in their place. To the exten t
  12| achievable, the APDC shall be comprised of the following persons: a member of the Lunenburg Historical Commission; a
  13| design professional; a craftsperson or building contractor familiar with historic restoration; and two residents of the APD and
  14| one resident at large. Members and alternates of the APDC shall by reason of experience and/or education have
  15| demonstrable knowledge, interest, and ability to effectively carry out the APDC’s responsibilities.
  16| Section 5: APDC Powers and Duties
  17| The APDC shall exercise its powers in reviewing the alteration (substantial or minor) , including demolition of contributing
  18| properties within the APD as set forth in this bylaw, and in accordance with the standards described in Section 9. Within 180
  19| days of its establishment, the APDC shall adopt design guidelines to assist the APDC in determining whether the proposal
  20| satisfies the standards described in Section 9, as well as to facilitate owner understanding of, and compliance with, those
  21| standards. The APDC may also promulgate rules and regulations consistent with the provisions of this bylaw, or set forth
  22| such forms and procedures as it deems appropriate for the regulation of its affairs and the conduct of its business, including
  23| requirements for the contents and form of applications for certificates, hearing procedures and other matters. In adopting
  24| design guidelines and rules and regulations, including any subsequent amendments, the APDC shall first hold a duly posted
  25| public hearing advertised at least fourteen (14) days in advance within a newspaper of local circulation.
  26| The APDC shall not adopt or amend any design guidelines or rules and regula tions until at least 35 days after the APDC has
  27| provided a copy of those proposed guidelines or rules and regulations to the Board of Selectmen.
  28| Section 6: Exemptions
  29| The following are exempt from the requirements of this bylaw:
  30| a) Routine maintenance of Contributing Properties.
  31| b) Repairs or improvements which do not require a building permit from the Building Inspector.
  32| c) Temporary buildings (subject to time and size limits imposed by the APDC).
  33| d) Interior building alterations.
  34| e) Exterior building colors.
  35| f) Installation, removal or replacement of storm windows and storm doors, screen windows and screen doors.
  36| g) Installation, removal or replacement of gutters, downspouts, window shutters and door shutters.
  37| h) Structures not defined as Contributing Properties.
  38| Section 7: Administrative Review/Determination of Applicability
  39| Minor alterations such as like for like replacement of architectural features and other minor work (as outlined in the APDC
  40| regulations) shall be reviewed through this process to ensure consistency of the property’s character and appropriateness of
  41| the proposed materials. The APDC shall make a determination that the proposed work is exempt or that it does not retain the
  42| character of the Contributing Property. In the case where the work does not retain the character of the Contributing Property
  43| it shall be determined to equate to a substantial alteration and require a binding review per Section 8 of this Bylaw.
  44| Property owners considering substantial alterations to a Contributing Property are strongly encouraged, but not required, to
  45| request an Administrative Review from the APDC. This review would focus on directing the applicant toward developing a
  46| proposal that would be compliant with the Binding Review Process in Section 8 .
  47| Administrative Review does not require a public hearing and can be requested electronically. In providing Administrative
  48| Review for a proposed Alteration, the APDC shall make a finding as to whether or not the proposed Alteration is compatible
  49| with the standards in Section 9 and the associated design guidelines. The APDC shall provide its finding in writing, as a
  50| Negative Determination or a Requirement of Binding Review, and within 45 days of receiving a request for Administrative
  51| Review. The Requirement of Binding Review may include recommendations on how the proposed Alteration could be made
  52| more consistent with the standards and the design guidelines.
  53| Section 8: Binding Review
  54| The following Alterations require the submittal of an application for binding review by the APDC:
  55| (a) Demolition of all or a part of a Contributing Property.

===PAGE 147=== (plain, 56 lines)
   1| 147
   2| (b) Substantial Alterations to a Contributing Property.
   3| Applications should include a completed application form and photographs showing existing buildings and site cond itions.
   4| For substantial alteration, including additions and demolition, applicants should also provide building elevations showing th e
   5| proposed configuration and building materials; a plan showing the approximate footprint and relationships of buildings to
   6| other structures, exterior circulation, and points of entry; and any other plans which may help to demonstrate the proposed
   7| building design. Applications can be submitted electronically, but applicants shall also provide one hard copy of the
   8| application to the Planning Office. If appropriate, the APDC may waive any or all of the requirements for design submittal
   9| and review.
  10| Within forty-five (45) days of the application submittal, a copy of which shall be filed with the Town Clerk, the APDC shall
  11| hold a public hearing on the application, said hearing to be noticed by an ad published at least once in a newspaper with local
  12| circulation fourteen (14) days or more before the hearing. Following the hearing, the APDC shall determine whether or not
  13| the proposed Alteration is consistent with the standards set forth in Section 9 and the associated design guidelines. If the
  14| APDC determines by majority vote that the Alteration adheres to the standards and the design guidelines, it shall issue a
  15| Certificate to Alter, which may be subject to reasonable conditions that the APDC deems necessary and appropriate. If the
  16| APDC does not achieve a majority vote that the Alteration is compatible with the standards and the design guidelines, it shal l
  17| make a negative determination. The APDC’s written determination shall be submitted to the property owner within twenty
  18| (20) days of the close of the public hearing, and no later than ninety (90) days after the submittal of an application, unles s
  19| granted a time extension by the owner. In making its determination, the APDC shall provide a rationale for its action,
  20| including a description of how the alteration does or does not meet the standards set forth in Section 9 and the design
  21| guidelines. If the APDC denies a Certificate to Alter, it may include non- binding advisory only, recommendations on how
  22| the proposed Alterations may be modified to satisfy the APDC. Once the applicant modifies the Alterations in a manner that
  23| the APDC finds acceptable, the APDC shall issue a Certificate to Alter. The determinations of the APDC relative to
  24| Alterations described in this Section 8, whether positive or negative, shall be binding on the applicant.
  25| Section 9: Design Standards When reviewing an application, the APDC shall consider the following standards, which are
  26| intended to guide the property owner in the site development and building design, as well as the APDC in its review of
  27| proposed actions as described in Section 8 above:
  28| (a) Height and Proportions – The height, proportions, and relationship of height to width between windows, doors, signs
  29| and other architectural elements should be compatible with the architectural style and character of the building or structure .
  30| (b) Relation of Structures and Spaces – The relation of a structure to the open space between it and adjoining structures
  31| should be compatible with such relationships in the district.
  32| (c) Shape – The shape of roofs, windows, doors and other design elements should be compatible with the architectural style
  33| and character of the building.
  34| (d) Scale – The scale of a structure alteration should be compatible with its architectural design style and character and that of
  35| the district.
  36| (e) Directional Expression – Building facades and other architectural design elements should be compatible with those of
  37| others in the district with regard to the dominant vertical or horizontal expression or direction related to use and historic al or
  38| cultural character, as appropriate.
  39| (f) Garages and Accessory Buildings – Garages and accessory buildings shall be sensitively integrated into the overall
  40| development, and should not be the predominant design feature when viewed from the street.
  41| (g) Materials & Methods of Construction – Materials used shall be consistent with the appearance and character of the
  42| surrounding properties and contribute to historic nature of the area. Construction methods shall meet Building Code
  43| requirements and visually resemble historic patterns but may use contemporary technology.
  44| Section 10: Procedures for Issuance and Filing of APDC Determinations: Each Certificate to Alter or negative
  45| determination issued by the APDC shall be dated and signed by its chair or such other person designated by the APDC to
  46| sign certificates on its behalf. The APDC shall send a written copy of its actions to the property owner and shall file other
  47| copies with the office of the Town Clerk and the Building Inspector. The date of issuance of a certificate or negative
  48| determination shall be the date of its filing of other said copy with the office of the  Town Clerk. If the APDC should fail to
  49| submit a written determination within twenty (20) days of closing its public hearing, or within ninety (90) days of the
  50| submittal of a Certificate, or within such further time as the applicant may allow in writing, a  Certificate to Alter shall be
  51| waived in favor of the property owner.
  52| Section 11: Enforcement In addition to the duties previously set forth in this Bylaw, the APDC or the Building Inspector
  53| shall issue enforcement orders, with an immediate cease and desist order directing compliance with this Bylaw and shall
  54| undertake any other enforcement authorized by law.
  55| Upon request of APDC, and with the approval of the Board of Selectmen, the Town Counsel shall take legal action on behalf
  56| of the Town for enforcement of this Bylaw, or any order issued hereunder, in any court of competent jurisdiction.

===PAGE 148=== (plain, 47 lines)
   1| 148
   2| In the case of demolition, including demolition by neglect, without approval of the APDC no building permit shall be issued
   3| with respect to any Contributing Property within the APD that caused a demolition of its building, structure or Contributing
   4| Property for a period of up to two (2) years after the date of the violation.
   5| The APDC or its designee may use any and all of the aforementioned enforcement methods to address the contributing
   6| factors of demolition by neglect for purposes of preventing a Contributing Property from becoming structurally deficient or
   7| economically infeasible to repair.
   8| Section 12: Coordination with Other Town Boards and Bylaws
   9| (a) Except as stated in “(b)” below, the APDC’s authority as established in this bylaw shall not supersede or interfere with the
  10| regulatory authority of other local or state boards or APDCs.
  11| (b) By exercise of this bylaw, the APDC assumes the authority of the Lunenburg His torical Commission pursuant to Chapter
  12| XVI of these General Bylaws, relative to the proposed demolition (including demolition by neglect) of buildings that are over
  13| 75 years of age and located within the APD.
  14| (c) The APDC shall coordinate its review with that of other Town Boards.
  15| (d) The APDC shall be included as an advisory review entity in new construction projects that move through the Planning
  16| Board Development Plan Review and/or Special Permit processes.
  17| Section 13: Appeal Procedure
  18| Any aggrieved party may appeal the action of the APDC in accordance with the General Laws.
  19| Section 14: Validity and Separability
  20| The provisions of this bylaw shall be deemed to be separable. If any of its provisions, sections, subsections, sentences or
  21| clauses shall be held to be invalid or unconstitutional by any court of competent jurisdiction, the remainder of this bylaw
  22| shall continue to be in full force and effect.
  23| Section 15: Appendices
  24| Appendix A: The location and boundaries of the Town of Lunenburg Architectural Preservation District are defined and
  25| shown on the Town of Lunenburg Village Center District Map and the Town of Lunenburg Historic District Map, which is a
  26| part of this bylaw.” or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted by the Historical Commission and the Planning
  27| Board) Planning Board recommends approval 5-0, Board of Selectmen recommends approval 5-0, Finance Committee reports no
  28| direct financial impact.
  29| ARTICLE 26.   VOTED (with amendments italicized) to amend the General Bylaws of the Town by adding
  30| Section XXXII, entitled Architectural Preservation District, as follows:
  31| Section XXXII ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION DISTRICT
  32| Section 1: Purpose
  33| This bylaw is enacted under the Home Rule Amendment of the Massachusetts Constitution for the pu rposes of:
  34| (a)  Preserving, restoring and protecting groups of historically and/or architecturally significant buildings and the
  35| characteristics of their neighborhoods that are important to the town’s architectural, cultural, economic, political and/or s ocial
  36| history;
  37| (b)  Fostering wider public knowledge and appreciation of such neighborhoods and Contributing Properties;
  38| (c)  Limiting the detrimental effect of alterations (substantial & minor), additions, and demolitions on the character of such
  39| Contributing Properties and their neighborhood settings;
  40| (d) Reviewing proposed alterations (substantial and minor) including demolition of or to any Contributing Property located
  41| within the APD, for appropriateness and compatibility with the existing buildings, setting and neighborhood character; and
  42| (e) Facilitating the protection of the APD through a regulatory review.
  43| To achieve these purposes, the Town of Lunenburg may designate APDs to be administered as set forth in this bylaw.
  44| Section 2: Definitions
  45| For the purpose of this bylaw, the terms and words listed below shall have the following meaning:
  46| ADDITION: Any addition to a principal or accessory Contributing Property that is enclosed by walls and which increases
  47| the existing gross floor area.

===PAGE 149=== (plain, 41 lines)
   1| 149
   2| ADMINISTRATIVE REVIEW/DETERMINATION OF APPLICABILITY: Pursuant to Section 7 of this bylaw, a
   3| process to review proposed alterations to Contributing Properties and identify the need for Binding Review.
   4|  Remove AGGRIEVED PARTY: The owner of the subject property or an owner of real property located within the APD or
   5| within three hundred feet of the subject property.
   6| ALTERATION, MINOR: Replacement, modification or material change to existing architectural features on a Contributing
   7| Property, which retains the character of the property as determined by the Architectural Preservation District Commission.
   8| Examples include, but are not limited to windows, doors, roof material and trim work.
   9| ALTERATION, SUBSTANTIAL: A change to a Contributing Property or part thereof, such as removal, reconstruction,
  10| restoration, replication, rehabilitation, addition, or demolition; and/or a change to a site that includes, enlarging or movi ng a
  11| building and other similar activities.
  12| ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION DISTRICT (“APD”):  An area of historic and /or architectural significance
  13| determined by the Town’s residents to be worthy of preservation.
  14| BINDING REVIEW: Pursuant to Section 8 of this bylaw, a mandatory process which applies to substantial alterations of
  15| contributing properties, including demolition, in the APD.
  16| BUILDING: A combination of materials having a roof, the purpose of which is the shelter of persons, animals, property, or
  17| processes, including any other permanent structure.
  18| CERTIFICATE TO ALTER: A document issued by the APDC allowing Alterations pursuant to Section 8 of this bylaw.
  19| CONTRIBUTING PROPERTY: Any building or stone wall within an APD, which is older than 75 years and contributes to
  20| the Architectural Preservation District’s historical character as determined by the  Architectural Preservation District
  21| Commission.
  22| DEMOLITION: The act of removing or razing structures or significant parts of structures, or commencing the work of total
  23| or substantial destruction with the intent of completing the same.
  24| DEMOLITION BY NEGLECT: Destruction by failure to maintain roof, siding, foundation or any other aspect that requires
  25| upkeep, which could ultimately result in the Contributing Property being condemned. These include but are not limited to:
  26| 1. Facades which may fall and injure members of the public or property.
  27| 2. Deteriorated or inadequate foundation, deteriorated flooring or floor supports, deteriorated walls or other vertical
  28| structural supports.
  29| 3. Members of ceilings, roofs, ceiling and roof supports or other horizontal members wh ich sag, split or buckle due to
  30| deterioration.
  31| 4. Deteriorated or ineffective waterproofing of exterior walls, roof, foundations or floors, including broken windows or
  32| doors.
  33| 5. Improper or insufficient waterproofing or exterior wall covering, including lack of paint or weathering due to lack of
  34| paint or other protective covering.
  35| 6. Any fault or defect in the Contributing Property caused by alteration or neglect of maintenance, which renders it not
  36| properly watertight or structurally unsafe.
  37| 7. Fireplaces or chimneys which list, bulge or settle due to deterioration.
  38| DESIGN GUIDELINES: A public document which provides further detail and illustration of the design standards
  39| established in Section 9 of this bylaw. Such guidelines, by facilitating the APDC’s determination as to whether proposed
  40| alterations are appropriate and compatible with the existing Contributing Property’s, setting and neighborhood character of
  41| the APD, will ensure fair, reasonable and objective preservation practices by the APDC.

  13| achievable, the APDC shall be comprised of the following persons: a member of the Lunenburg Historical Commission; a

   8| a) Routine maintenance of Contributing Properties.

  11| d) Interior building alterations.
  12| e) Exterior building colors.

  14| g) Installation, removal or replacement of gutters, downspouts, window shutters and door shutters.
  15| h) Structures not defined as Contributing Properties.
  16| Section 7: Administrative Review/Determination of Applicability
  17| Minor alterations such as like for like replacement of architectural features and other minor work (as outlined in the APDC
  18| regulations) shall be reviewed through this process to ensure consistency of the property’s character and appropriateness of
  19| the proposed materials. The APDC shall make a determination that the proposed work is exempt or that it does not retain the
  20| character of the Contributing Property. In the case where the work does not retain the character of the Contributing Property
  21| it shall be determined to equate to a substantial alteration and require a binding review per Section 8 of this Bylaw.
  22| Property owners considering substantial alterations to a Contributing Property are strongly encouraged, but not required, to
  23| request an Administrative Review from the APDC. This review would focus on directing the applicant toward developing a
  24| proposal that would be compliant with the Binding Review Process in Section 8.  Administrative Review does not require a
  25| public hearing and can be requested electronically. In providing Administrative Re view for a proposed Alteration, the APDC
  26| shall make a finding as to whether or not the proposed Alteration is compatible with the standards in Section 9 and the
  27| associated design guidelines. The APDC shall provide its finding in writing, as a Negative Dete rmination or a Requirement

  29| Review may include recommendations on how the proposed Alteration could be made more consistent with the standards  and
  30| the design guidelines.
  31| Section 8: Binding Review
  32| The following Alterations require the submittal of an application for binding review by the APDC:
  33| (a) Demolition of all or a part of a Contributing Property.
  34| (b) Substantial Alterations to a Contributing Property.
  35| Applications should include a completed application form and photographs showing existing buildings and site conditions.
  36| For substantial alteration, including additions and demolition, applicants should also provide building elevations sho wing the
  37| proposed configuration and building materials; a plan showing the approximate footprint and relationships of buildings to
  38| other structures, exterior circulation, and points of entry; and any other plans which may help to demonstrate the proposed
  39| building design. Applications can be submitted electronically, but applicants shall also provide one hard copy of the
  40| application to the Planning Office. If appropriate, the APDC may waive any or all of the requirements for design submittal
  41| and review.
  42| Within thirty (45) days of the application submittal, a copy of which shall be filed with the Town Clerk, the APDC shall hold
  43| a public hearing on the application, said hearing to be noticed by an ad published at least once in a newspaper with local
  44| circulation fourteen (14) days or more before the hearing. Following the hearing, the APDC shall determine whether or not
  45| the proposed Alteration is consistent with the standards set forth in Section 9 and the associated design guidelines. If the
  46| APDC determines by majority vote that the Alteration adheres to the standards and the design guidelines, it shall issue a
  47| Certificate to Alter, which may be subject to reasonable conditions that the APDC deems necessary and appropriate. If the
  48| APDC does not achieve a majority vote that the Alteration is compatible with the standards and the design guidelines, it shall
  49| make a negative determination. The APDC’s written determination shall be submitted to the property owner within twenty
  50| (20) days of the close of the public hearing, and no later than ninety (90) days after the submittal of an application, unless
  51| granted a time extension by the owner. In making its determination, the APDC shall provide a rationale for its action,
  52| including a description of how the alteration does or does not meet the standards set forth in Section 9 and the design
  53| guidelines. If the APDC denies a Certificate to Alter, it may include non - binding advisory only, recommendations on how
  54| the proposed Alterations may be modified to satisfy the APDC. Once the applicant modifies the Alterations in a manner that
  55| the APDC finds acceptable, the APDC shall issue a Certificate to Alter. The determinations of the APDC relative to
  56| Alterations described in this Section 8, whether positive or negative, shall be bind ing on the applicant.

   1| 153
   2| Section 9: Design Standards When reviewing an application, the APDC shall consider the following standards, which are
   3| intended to guide the property owner in the site development and building design, as well as the APDC in its revie w of
   4| proposed actions as described in Section 8 above:
   5| (a) Height and Proportions – The height, proportions, and relationship of height to width between windows, doors, signs
   6| and other architectural elements should be compatible with the architectural style and character of the building or structure.
   7| (b) Relation of Structures and Spaces – The relation of a structure to the open space between it and adjoining structures
   8| should be compatible with such relationships in the district.
   9| (c) Shape – The shape of roofs, windows, doors and other design elements should be compatible with the architectural style
  10| and character of the building.
  11| (d) Scale – The scale of a structure alteration should be compatible with its architectural design style and character and t hat of
  12| the district.
  13| (e) Directional Expression – Building facades and other architectural design elements should be compatible with those of
  14| others in the district with regard to the dominant vertical or horizontal expression or direction related to use and historical or
  15| cultural character, as appropriate.
  16| (f) Garages and Accessory Buildings – Garages and accessory buildings shall be sensitively integrated into the overall
  17| development, and should not be the predominant design feature when viewed from the  street.
  18| (g) Materials & Methods of Construction – Materials used shall be consistent with the appearance and character of the
  19| surrounding properties and contribute to historic nature of the area. Construction methods shall meet Building Code
  20| requirements and visually resemble historic patterns but may use contemporary technology.
  21| Section 10: Procedures for Issuance and Filing of APDC Determinations: Each Certificate to Alter or negative
  22| determination issued by the APDC shall be dated and signed by its chair or such other person designated by the APDC to
  23| sign certificates on its behalf. The APDC shall send a written copy of its actions to the property owner and shall file other
  24| copies with the office of the Town Clerk and the Building Inspector. The date of  issuance of a certificate or negative
  25| determination shall be the date of its filing of other said copy with the office of the Town Clerk. If the APDC should fail t o
  26| submit a written determination within twenty (20) days of closing its public hearing, or w ithin ninety (90) days of the
  27| submittal of a Certificate, or within such further time as the applicant may allow in writing, a Certificate to Alter shall b e
  28| waived in favor of the property owner.
  29| Section 11: Enforcement In addition to the duties previously set forth in this Bylaw, the APDC or the Building Inspector
  30| shall issue enforcement orders, with an immediate cease and desist order directing compliance with this Bylaw and shall
  31| undertake any other enforcement authorized by law.
  32| Upon request of APDC, and with the approval of the Board of Selectmen, the Town Counsel shall take legal action on behalf
  33| of the Town for enforcement of this Bylaw, or any order issued hereunder, in any court of competent jurisdiction.
  34| In the case of demolition, including demolition by neglect, without approval of the APDC no building permit shall be issued
  35| with respect to any Contributing Property within the APD that caused a demolition of its building, structure or Contributing
  36| Property for a period of up to two (2) years after the date of the violation.
  37| The APDC or its designee may use any and all of the aforementioned enforcement methods to address the contributing
  38| factors of demolition by neglect for purposes of preventing a Contributing Property from becoming structurally de ficient or
  39| economically infeasible to repair.
  40| Section 12: Coordination with Other Town Boards and Bylaws
  41| (a) Except as stated in “(b)” below, the APDC’s authority as established in this bylaw shall not supersede or interfere with the
  42| regulatory authority of other local or state boards or APDCs.
  43| (b) By exercise of this bylaw, the APDC assumes the authority of the Lunenburg Historical Commission pursuant to Chapter
  44| XVI of these General Bylaws, relative to the proposed demolition (including demolition by n eglect) of buildings that are over
  45| 75 years of age and located within the APD.
  46| (c) The APDC shall coordinate its review with that of other Town Boards.
  47| (d) The APDC shall be included as an advisory review entity in new construction projects that move thr ough the Planning
  48| Board Development Plan Review and/or Special Permit processes.
  49| Section 13: Appeal Procedure
  50| Any aggrieved party may appeal the action of the APDC in accordance with the General Laws.
  51| Section 14: Validity and Separability
  52| The provisions of this bylaw shall be deemed to be separable. If any of its provisions, sections, subsections, sentences or
  53| clauses shall be held to be invalid or unconstitutional by any court of competent jurisdiction, the remainder of this bylaw
  54| shall continue to be in full force and effect.

   1| 154
   2| Section 15: Appendices
   3| Appendix A: The location and boundaries of the Town of Lunenburg Architectural Preservation District are defined and
   4| shown on the Town of Lunenburg Village Center District Map and the Town of Lunenburg H istoric District Map, which is a
   5| part of this bylaw.”
   6| ARTICLE 27.  To see if the Town will vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw by adding Section 4.17, Village Center
   7| District as follows:
   8| 4.17. VILLAGE CENTER DISTRICT
   9| PREAMBLE
  10| The Lunenburg Town Center region has tremendous importance in symbolizing the unique character and heritage of
  11| Lunenburg.  As residents engage with the historic buildings and streetscape of this area in the course of their routine daily
  12| business, be it school related, recreational, commercial, or even just driving through, the experience greatly reinforces a
  13| profound bond with the town’s present community and past heritage.
  14| There is an opportunity to significantly improve the quality of this already poignant civic experience and increas e the number
  15| of people who will be drawn to share in it by expanding the businesses and other attractions in the area.  There is also a ke y
  16| challenge to preserving this critical embodiment of our town. Appropriate new uses or remedies must be found to the
  17| increasing number of underutilized buildings located about the town center.
  18| Town planning experience and best practices, as exemplified in the state’s model bylaw for Village Center Districts,
  19| demonstrates some key ingredients to sustaining and enriching this type of environment in small towns.  One of these is
  20| careful stewardship through clear guidelines for development consistent with what the area means to the community.
  21| Another is encouraging a level of intensive mixed use development, so that the c ritical mass of social and economic activity
  22| can be reached that will naturally provide the resources and inspiration required to maintain and organically adapt the area
  23| landmarks, buildings, and infrastructure without excessive burden on the Town’s public  finances.
  24| 4.17.1. Purpose
  25| In pursuit of this opportunity and to address this challenge, the Town implements this bylaw and designates this zoning
  26| district as Village Center District in order to encourage economic and residential growth that fits the chara cter of the Town.
  27| The purposes of the Village Center District are to:
  28| a. Build upon the historic development patterns in the existing village centers to create attractive, walkable neighborhoods;
  29| b. Encourage adaptive reuse of abandoned, vacant or underutilized buildings or structures where appropriate;
  30| c. Allow for a mix of new land uses that are appropriate to both the needs of the community and the scale of surrounding
  31| neighborhoods;
  32| d. Provide incentives to develop larger parcels at higher densities and in a coordinated, planned approach;
  33| e. Maintain a consistently high level of design quality throughout the district;
  34| f. Encourage the development of mixed use buildings and campuses that are designed and constructed in a manner that is
  35| contextually sensitive to the existing structures and facilities.
  36| 4.17.2. Establishment
  37| The Village Center District is hereby established as of 1 January 2016 and consists of the area shown as the Village Center
  38| District on Town of Lunenburg Zoning Map on file with the Town /City Clerk and dated 2 May 2015 as may be amended
  39| from time to time by Town Meeting.
  40| 4.17.3. Definitions
  41| Artist Space: A shop for the creation and/or sale of goods made by a potter, ceramicist, silversmith, jewelry maker, painter,
  42| photographer or other fine artist. This space may also serve as a living space for the artist provided there is adequate
  43| separation between uses per the governing Building and Health Codes.
  44| Banner: A strip of cloth or other man made fabric on which a sign is painted, silk -screened or printed. The display area shall
  45| be defined as the area of one face of the banner. The banner shall not exceed (40) square feet and shall not be displayed for
  46| more than fourteen (14) consecutive days.
  47| Bed & Breakfast:  Bed and Breakfast, or Bed and Breakfast Establishment provided that:
  48| a) The dwelling is owner occupied.
  49| b) The lot conforms to all dimensional requirements of the Zoning Bylaw.
  50| c) Parking for each room available for rent is provided in the side yard behind the setback line or in the rea r yard, but not
  51| nearer than ten (10) feet to any property line. One (1) parking place shall be provided for each bedroom.
  52| d) The outside appearance of the building is not altered from that of a residence, except to comply with the Building Code.

===PAGE 155=== (plain, 57 lines)
   1| 155
   2| Civic Use: A land use that provides a public, cultural, or institutional benefit to the community. Specific uses may include,
   3| but shall not be limited to, government offices, religious institutions, educational institutions, and medical facilities (no t
   4| including veterinary operations). For the purposes of this bylaw, civic uses shall not include open space as defined below.
   5| Multiple Commercial Use: A combination of commercial uses within a single building or lot.  In the case of uses that fall
   6| under the Special Permit requirements of Section 4.17.5.b. a Zoning Board of Appeals decision must be rendered on the use
   7| prior to the filing for Development Plan Review.
   8| Mixed Use: A combination of commercial and residential uses within a single building or lot. In the case of uses that fall
   9| under the Special Permit requirements of Section 4.17.5.b. a Zoning Board of Appeals decision must be rendered on the use
  10| prior to the filing for Development Plan Review.
  11| Personal Service: An establishment engaged in the provision of services to the general public which are conducted within the
  12| structure. This includes, but is not limited to, barbers, hair salons, massage therapists, chiropractors and nail salons.
  13| Professional Office: An office primarily devoted to professional activities, including, but not limited to, banks, real estate,
  14| insurance or other agency offices; an office of an accountant, physician, teacher, dentist, engineer, lawyer, homebuilder,
  15| photographer, or other recognized professional for which specific training and/or l icensing is required. Drive-thru service
  16| shall be limited to banks and shall require a Special Permit.
  17| Open Space: In the context of an application for a Village Center District Special Permit, this term denotes open areas set
  18| aside for public use as part of a coordinated site development process. Specific requirements for ownership and maintenance
  19| are provided in Section 4.17.9. and shall not include areas covered with impervious surface treatments.
  20| Transparency: The amount of transparent space that occupies a building façade including standard street-level windows and
  21| doorway windows.
  22| Restaurant: A place serving food and beverage within a structure or patio, primarily for consumption on the premises. Drive -
  23| thru windows shall be prohibited for this use.
  24| Retail: An establishment engaged in displaying and selling goods or merchandise within a building to the general public or
  25| to business establishments where the goods or merchandise are not intended for resale. There shall be no outdoor display of
  26| goods without the granting of Special Permit from the Board of Appeals.
  27| 4.17.4. Authority
  28| The Planning Board shall act as the administering authority for any Development Plan Review procedure associated with this
  29| bylaw Section 8.4. The Board of Appeals shall serve as the Special Permit Granting Authority for any use that requires a
  30| Special Permit in the district, any use requiring a Special Permit pursuant to Section 4.17.5.
  31| 4.17.5. Use Provisions
  32| a. The following uses are allowed by-right subject to any Development Plan Review requirements listed in Section 8.4 and all
  33| applicable density and design provisions listed in this bylaw.
  34| 1) Retail, with maximum 5,000 sq. ft. of floor area
  35| 2) Professional Office Space
  36| 3) Personal Service
  37| 4) Artist space
  38| 5) Restaurant
  39| 6) Bed & Breakfast
  40| 7) Mixed Use containing less than  5,000 sq. ft. per individual proposed use
  41| 8) Multiple Commercial Use containing less than 5,000 sq. ft.  per individual proposed use
  42| 9) Civic Uses
  43| b. The following uses are allowed only through the granting of a Special Permit by the Bo ard of Appeals pursuant to the
  44| procedures outlined in Section 8.3.
  45| 1) Single and Two-Family Homes
  46| 2) Multi-Family Homes
  47| 3) Apartment Complexes
  48| 4) Movie House/ Theatre (maximum of two screens)
  49| 5) Liquor Stores
  50| 6) Outdoor Markets subject to applicable licensing requirements
  51| 7) Private Club
  52| 8) Drive Through Use for Banks
  53| 9) Retail Use greater than 5,000 sq. ft.
  54| 10) Uses allowed by -right with a floor area greater than 5,000 sq. ft. included in a Multiple Commercial Use or Mixed
  55| Use development.
  56| c. Special Permit Approval Criteria – The Zoning Board of Appeals shall act upon all applications a fter notice and public
  57| hearing, and after due consideration may grant such a special permit provided that it finds the proposed use:

===PAGE 156=== (plain, 44 lines)
   1| 156
   2| a. Is in harmony with the purpose and intent of the Bylaw and will promote the purposes of the Village Center District.
   3| b. The use is in harmony with those of the abutting properties and the Village Center District as a whole.
   4| c. All applicable standards for use, parking, and dimensional requirements are met;
   5| d. All applicable design standards listed in Section 8 are met.
   6| e. Where multiple structures are proposed, the site design reflects a thoughtful arrangement of elements that will facilitate th e
   7| movement of pedestrians between structures through the use of sidewalks, in ternal walkways, alleys or open space features.
   8| d. The following uses are prohibited in the Village Center District
   9| 1) Newly constructed one-story buildings on lots of less than 5 acres.
  10| 2) Retail operations with more than five thousand (5,000) square feet of gross floor area on any individual floor, except for
  11|      reuse of buildings existing prior to 1 May 2015.
  12| 3) Drive through establishments, excluding those expressly allowed by Special Permit.
  13| 4.17.6. Dimensional Requirements
  14| a.

  26| Minimum Lot Area N/A
  27| Number of Buildings Per Lot See Section 4.17.9

  38| b. Height Limitations for Non -residential and Mixed Use: Building height for mixed use or non -residential use shall not
  39| exceed thirty-eight (38) feet and no building shall have more than three (3) stories.
  40| 4.17.7. Parking Requirements
  41| Use Parking Requirement
  42| Retail/Personal Service/Liquor Store  1 parking space per 400 sq. ft. of net floor area, plus 1
  43| space per employee on the largest shift
  44| Professional Office/Civic Uses 1 parking space per 300 sq. ft. of net floor area

===PAGE 157=== (plain, 42 lines)
   1| 157
   2| Artists Space 1 parking space per 400 sq. ft. of net retail/gallery floor
   3| area, plus 2 parking space per dwelling unit
   4| Restaurant/Private Club 1 parking space per 4 seats, plus one for each employee
   5| on the largest shift
   6| Bed & Breakfast 1 parking space per accommodation room, plus 2
   7| parking spaces for the primary residence, plus 1 per non-
   8| resident employee on the largest shift.
   9| Mixed Use/Multiple Commercial Parking to be Provided per the Individual Uses
  10| All Residential Uses 2 parking spaces per dwelling unit
  11| Movie House 1 parking space per 4 occupants, plus 1 parking space
  12| per 2 employees on the largest shift
  13| Outdoor Market 1 parking space per 500 sq. ft.
  14| As part of a Development Plan approval or Special Permit process within this district, the applicant may request reductions to
  15| minimum requirements or alternative methods for meeting the required parking. Available innovative parking strategies
  16| include:
  17| a. Shared On-Site Parking
  18| 1) Non-competing Uses. In mixed -use developments, applicants may pr opose a reduction in parking requirements
  19| based on an analysis of peak demands for noncompeting uses. Up to 50% of the requirements for the predominant
  20| use may be waived by the Planning Board or the Board of Appeals if the applicant can demonstrate that th e peak
  21| demands for two uses do not overlap. An additional 25% may be waived with the addition of bicycle parking
  22| facilities.
  23| b. Off-Site Parking
  24| Separate from, or in conjunction with Shared Parking provisions, an applicant may use off -site parking to sati sfy their
  25| parking requirements in accordance with the following conditions:
  26| 1) Off-site parking shall be within one-thousand (1000) feet of the property for which it is being requested.
  27| 2) Off -site parking spaces provided by a separate private property own er shall be subject to a legally binding
  28| agreement that will be presented to the Permit Granting Authority as a condition of either the Development Plan
  29| Review or the Special Permit. Where an agreement shall expire within a specified timeline, the applican t or current
  30| property owner shall continue to provide evidence to the Zoning Enforcement Agent that the agreement has been
  31| extended. The Permit Granting Authority will verify with the Zoning Enforcement Officer that there are no existing
  32| parking agreements during the permitting process.
  33| 4.17.8. Design Standards
  34| The Design Standards in this section shall be applied to development within the Village Center District where applicable.
  35| a. Occupied Lot Area.
  36|      1) The total lot devoted to building, parking, outdoor storage and display and other impervious surfaces may occupy up to
  37|          eighty-five (85%) percent of the total lot area.
  38| b. Buildings
  39|     1) All buildings shall have a principal façade and entry (with operable doors) facing a stree t or open space. Buildings may
  40|         have more than one principal façade and/or entry.
  41|     2) Building finish materials shall be appropriate to traditional New England architecture.
  42|     3) Building facades, materials and roof lines shall be reviewed under the permit granting authority to ensure consistency

===PAGE 158=== (plain, 58 lines)
   1| 158
   2|         and compatibility with other structures.
   3|     4) Blank walls adjacent to streets, alleys or open spaces shall not be permitted. Whe re windows are not possible or
   4|         appropriate to the intended use, vertical articulation in the form of raised or recessed surfaces shall be used to break up
   5|         blank walls.
   6|     5) New retail buildings shall have one of the following features along the front entrances to pedestrians: awning, marquee,
   7|         arcade and/ or colonnade.
   8|     6) Flat roofs that are visible from the street are prohibited unless an appropriate façade is included in the design.
   9|     7) Larger buildings with multiple non-residential tenants on the first floor shall articulate the façade in a manner that
  10|         distinguishes the location of these tenants through the use of decorative raised or depressed vertical surfaces, variations
  11|         of acceptable signage, awnings, marquees, colonnades or arcades.
  12|     8) Mixed Use buildings shall have no more than twenty five (25%) percent of the first floor dedicated to residential use.
  13|     Conversion or partial conversion of residential uses existing before 1 May 2015 are exempt from this provision.
  14|     9) All new utility service connections shall be placed underground.
  15|     10) Lighting, signage and architectural style shall be consistent with other uses in the District and reviewed under the
  16|           Permit Granting Authority.
  17| c. Signs
  18| 1) Primary signs shall be flat against the façade, or mounted projecting from the façade.
  19| 2) Signs that project from buildings shall have at least ten (10) feet of clearance from the ground level.
  20| 3) Signs shall be externally lit from the front; lighting shall be provided using a gooseneck fixture. Back li ghting of signs
  21| shall not be used.
  22| 4) Neon, flashing signs, moving signs, electric message signs, and roof signs shall not be used, except for barber poles.
  23| 5) Banners with a specific date of expiration, shall be allowed, after approval by the Zoning Enfor cement Officer.
  24| 6) Signs shall be made of attractive materials consistent with the character of the district. Materials may include wood
  25|     (painted or natural), stone, copper, brass, galvanized steel, painted canvas or paint/engraved on façade surface.
  26| 7) Signs may only be incorporated into the skirt of awnings and not on the primary angled surface.
  27| 8) Each building façade facing a street or parking area is permitted to have signs that equal a maximum square footage of fiv e
  28|     (5%) percent of the respective façade.
  29| 9) A building with multiple businesses shall have signs of equal size on the façade, signs shall be either rectangular or ova l
  30|     shaped.
  31| 10) Free standing signs shall be prohibited except for municipal use, portable and temporary signs.
  32| 11) Sandwich board signs, flags and other portable signs are permitted only within the property lines, as long as they are
  33|       properly weighted.
  34| 12) Window signs, comprising no more than ten (10%) percent of the transparent surface, shall be allowed. Window signs
  35|        shall not be lighted or animated in any manner, with the exception of the standard lighting fixtures within the building.
  36| d. Site Design
  37|     1)  Interior Streets, Drives, Walkways and Access.
  38|          a) Site access shall be a single divided way (one way in and one way out) where and when appropriate as determined by
  39|              the Permit Granting Authority.
  40|          b) Surfaces shall be pervious when possible and practical.
  41|          c) Street level frontage shall be devoted to entrances, shop windows or other displays.
  42|          d) Primary entrances to proposed and existing buildings shall be situated on pedestrian amenities (e.g., sidewalks,
  43|              plazas or open space) with a minimum width of 10 feet.
  44|          e) Setbacks shall be consistent with the fabric of the existing street and do not preclude pedestrian access.
  45|   2) Parking and Loading Area.
  46|        a) Parking shall be in the rear or side of building(s) and shall not be visible from the street line when possible. Par king
  47|            will be reviewed by the Permit Granting Authority.
  48|        b) All loading docks shall be to the rear of the building(s) and shall not be visible from the street. Adequate access for
  49|             loading and emergency vehicles is maintained on one side of the building.
  50|   3) Sidewalks.
  51|        a) Sidewalks shall be provided from the street line, when applicable, and from the parking areas to building(s).
  52|   4) Landscaping.
  53|       a) There shall be a minimum of a four (4) foot landscaped area along the street frontage and along the front and side of
  54|           the principal building(s) and plantings of indigenous materials along the façades of the building(s) and between the
  55|           building(s) if there is more than one principal building on site.
  56|       b) Additional landscaping and screening may be required during the Development Plan Review or by the permitting
  57|           authority.
  58|       c) Such landscape shall be a type and height that does not interfere with sight lines of drivers.

===PAGE 159=== (plain, 54 lines)
   1| 159
   2|       d) Natural features shall be retained to the extent possible.
   3|       e) A landscape plan shall be provided and reviewed.
   4|       f) Where residential neighborhoods abut commercial, office or mixed use developments, appropriate transitional features
   5|          shall be used and may include landscaping, open space or parks, or streets with clearly designed pedestrian features.
   6| 5) Street Furniture.
   7|      a) Light fixtures shall be designed to be of number and height that grants plentiful lighting. Such lighting shall  shine
   8|          downwards as to not affect adjacent properties and be dark sky compliant.
   9|      b)  Lighting must also be placed on the side and rear of the building.
  10|      c) A Lighting Plan may be required if the Permitting Granting Authority deems the pr oject is of such a scale that the
  11|          impacts to the neighborhood are adverse; such plan will show where all light fixtures are being proposed within the site,
  12|          as well as a narrative as to the type of light and height of all fixtures.
  13|      d) Outdoor tables, benches, and bicycle racks shall be of a style consistent with the principal use(s) of the site.
  14|       e) The location, number and style of exterior trash receptacles shall be reviewed under Permit Granting Authority.
  15| 4.17.9. Village Center District Campus Developments
  16| Developments that contain multiple buildings and uses on a single lot are encouraged as long as they are sensitive to the
  17| context of the surrounding area and meet the purposes outlined above. These developments are subjec t to the provisions of
  18| Section 8.4 Development Plan Review of the Protective Bylaw as well as the additional design guidelines outlined in Section
  19| 4.17.8. of this Bylaw and below.
  20| a. Additional Design Standards: In addition to those design standards liste d in Section 4.17.8. of this bylaw, applications
  21| for a Village Center District Campus shall also meet the following standards:
  22| 1) Buildings
  23| i. Newly constructed building façades for non -residential first floor uses shall have a transparency of at least twenty
  24| (20%) percent and no more than sixty (60%) percent for all facades that, wholly or partially, address street frontage,
  25| sidewalks, or other public space.
  26| ii. Buildings in Village Center District Campus Developments shall meet the following criteria:
  27| a) No more t han ten (10%) percent of the first floor of newly constructed buildings shall be occupied by
  28| residential use.
  29| b) No more than sixty (60%) percent of the total gross floor area of the development shall be occupied by
  30| residential use.
  31| 2) Signs
  32| i. Freestanding directory signs may be permitted as part of a Village Center District Campus application where several
  33| non-residential operations are accessed through a common vehicular entrance. Such freestanding signs shall not
  34| exceed eight (8) feet in height, six (6) feet  in width and each tenant shall be allowed a maximum of four and a half
  35| (4.5) square feet to display the company or agency name.
  36| 3) Site Design
  37| i. Buildings shall be arranged in a manner that optimizes the ability of residents and consumers to access public spaces
  38| and pedestrian amenities.
  39| (ii) Buildings shall be oriented toward each other in a way that minimizes conflicts between pedestrians and
  40| automobiles.
  41|                (iii) Open space provided pursuant to Section 4.17.9.b. of this bylaw shall be designed as a public gathering place.
  42|                Arcades, courtyards, parks, greens or other common areas shall be located in a manner that connects buildings to
  43|                each other and to public sidewalks without interruption from parking areas or automobile travel lanes to the greatest
  44|                practicable extent.
  45|         (iv) Features that may be used to create open space areas acceptable to the Planning Board or the Board of Appeals
  46| may include, without limitation, fixed benches, fixed tabl es, fountains, pathways, bikeways, bicycle racks, period
  47| lighting, shade trees, perennial gardens, picnic areas, and/or trash receptacles.
  48| 4.17.10 Severability
  49| If any provision of this bylaw is held invalid by a court of competent jurisdiction, the remaind er of the bylaw shall not be
  50| affected thereby, or take any other action relative thereto    (Submitted by the Planning Board) Board of Selectmen
  51| recommends approval 5-0, Planning Board recommends approval 5-0, Finance Committee reports no direct financial
  52| impact..
  53| VOTED (2/3rds DECLARED) (with amendments italicized) that the Town vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw by adding
  54| Section 4.17, Village Center District as follows:
